北极圈内狗和野生动物 狂犬病 检测环境指标范围审查议定书

Protocol for a Scoping Review of Environmental Indicators of Rabies Detection in Dogs and Wildlife in the Circumpolar North
作者： Lauren Grant, Juliette Di Francesco, Laurence Daigle, Cole Heasley
摘要中文翻译该注册用于范围界定审查协议。本综述旨在解决以下研究问题:哪些环境指标是北极圈人类、狗和野生动物种群 狂犬病 风险增加的潜在预测因素?方法见附表。
查看研究七要素
问题与背景
本次范围界定审查旨在确定预测北极圈内人类、狗和野生动物种群 狂犬病 风险增加的环境指标,解决 狂犬病 狂犬病 和预防策略方面的知识差距。
设计与对象
该研究是一项范围审查协议,重点关注环极北地区狗和野生动物中 狂犬病 检测的环境指标。
核心方法
所附协议详细介绍了这些方法,该协议概述了用于识别和综合 狂犬病 风险环境指标证据的范围界定审查方法。
主要结果
该审查将解决以下研究问题:哪些环境指标是北极圈人类、狗和野生动物种群 狂犬病 风险增加的潜在预测因素。
解释与创新
该协议建立了一个系统框架来绘制环境 狂犬病 指标的现有证据,这对于环极北地区来说是新颖的。
科研与公卫意义
研究结果将通过确定北极圈内的关键环境风险因素,为有针对性的 狂犬病 监测 和预防策略提供信息。
局限与证据强度
证据强度仅限于协议阶段,实际结果有待范围界定审查完成。

Protocol for a Scoping Review of Environmental Indicators of Rabies Detection in Dogs and Wildlife in the Circumpolar North

Authors: Lauren Grant, Juliette Di Francesco, Laurence Daigle, Cole Heasley
Original abstract (English)
This registration is for the protocol of a scoping review. This review aims to address the following research question: what environmental indicators are potential predictors of an increased risk of rabies in human, dog, and wildlife populations in the Circumpolar North? See attached protocol for methods.
View research seven-element analysis
Question and background
This scoping review aims to identify environmental indicators that predict increased rabies risk in human, dog, and wildlife populations in the Circumpolar North, addressing a knowledge gap in rabies surveillance and prevention strategies.
Design and population
The study is a scoping review protocol with a focus on environmental indicators of rabies detection in dogs and wildlife across the Circumpolar North region.
Core methods
The methods are detailed in the attached protocol, which outlines the scoping review approach for identifying and synthesizing evidence on environmental indicators of rabies risk.
Main results
The review will address the research question of what environmental indicators are potential predictors of increased rabies risk in human, dog, and wildlife populations in the Circumpolar North.
Interpretation and novelty
The protocol establishes a systematic framework to map existing evidence on environmental rabies indicators, which is novel for the Circumpolar North context.
Scientific and public-health significance
The findings will inform targeted rabies surveillance and prevention strategies by identifying key environmental risk factors in the Circumpolar North.
Limitations and evidence strength
The evidence strength is limited to the protocol stage, with actual results pending the completion of the scoping review.

双阳性 (DP) 发育中的胸腺细胞调节胸腺内神经元细胞体的发育 2260542

Double positive (DP) developing thymocytes regulate the development of neuronal cell bodies within the thymus 2260542
作者： Katayoun Ayasoufi, Alexandra M. Hoyt-Miggelbrink, Christian Pfaller, Fang Jin, Eliese M. Moelker, Aaron J. Johnson, Pamela Norberg
摘要中文翻译摘要 介绍 各种器官的周围神经系统神经支配与免疫系统的发育和维持有关。然而,胸腺神经支配的复杂细节仍然没有得到充分研究。有趣的是,包括脑肿瘤和嗜神经感染在内的各种神经损伤已被证明对胸腺大小、细胞结构和 T 细胞发育具有深远影响。方法我们使用流式细胞术、共聚焦显微镜、神经示踪剂和基因敲除小鼠来评估胸腺内正在发育的 T 细胞-神经元相互作用。结果我们已经鉴定出胸腺内的神经元细胞体。这些细胞表达 NeuN 和酪氨酸羟化酶等神经元标记,但缺乏 EpCAM、UEA-1 和 Ly51 等 TEC 标识符。胸腺神经元可以被嗜神经 狂犬病 媒介 感染,进一步巩固其神经元身份。考虑到胸腺主要由 T 细胞组成,我们接下来研究了 T 细胞和胸腺神经元之间的潜在相互作用。有趣的是,T 细胞发育的丧失导致胸腺神经元发育缺乏,表明发育过程中 T 细胞-神经元的共同调节。然而,胸腺神经元可以在骨髓移植后恢复,这表明发育中的 T 细胞为神经元提供了分化和/或存活信号。使用在不同发育阶段缺乏 T 细胞谱系的基因敲除小鼠,我们确定胸腺神经元的发育需要 TCR β 选择。最后,我们确定缺乏从 DN4 到 DP 的过渡会严重削弱胸腺神经元的发育。对胸腺 DP 与 DN 细胞群进行比较的公开批量 RNA 测序数据的分析表明,IL34 可能参与胸腺神经元发育。结论 我们描述了胸腺内的一个新的神经元细胞群,它们与发育中的胸腺细胞相互作用以建立最佳的胸腺结构。资金来源 NIH K99/R00 主题类别 神经免疫学 (NEUR)
查看研究七要素
问题与背景
鉴于神经损伤会影响胸腺大小和 T 细胞发育,该研究旨在调查 T 细胞发育与胸腺内神经元细胞体之间的关系。
设计与对象
该研究使用流式细胞术、共聚焦显微镜、神经示踪剂和基因敲除小鼠来评估胸腺内的 T 细胞-神经元相互作用。
核心方法
采用流式细胞术、共聚焦显微镜、神经示踪剂和基因敲除小鼠来分析 T 细胞-神经元相互作用,并使用大量 RNA 测序数据来比较胸腺 DP 与 DN 细胞群。
主要结果
在胸腺内鉴定出表达 NeuN 和酪氨酸羟化酶的神经元细胞体,并发现它们的发育依赖于 TCR β 选择和从 DN4 到 DP 胸腺细胞的转变。
解释与创新
作者解释说,发育中的胸腺细胞调节胸腺神经元的发育,在胸腺内建立一个新的神经元细胞群,它们相互作用以优化胸腺结构。
科研与公卫意义
这些发现提供了对胸腺中神经免疫相互作用的见解,可能有助于了解神经系统疾病如何影响免疫发育,并为进一步研究胸腺相关疾病提供途径。
局限与证据强度
证据来自仅摘要的数据,虽然方法很稳健,但缺乏全文细节可能会限制对普遍性和潜在偏差的评估。

Double positive (DP) developing thymocytes regulate the development of neuronal cell bodies within the thymus 2260542

Authors: Katayoun Ayasoufi, Alexandra M. Hoyt-Miggelbrink, Christian Pfaller, Fang Jin, Eliese M. Moelker, Aaron J. Johnson, Pamela Norberg
Original abstract (English)
Abstract Introduction Peripheral nervous system innervation of various organs has been associated with the development and maintenance of the immune system. Yet, the intricate details of thymic innervation remain less well-studied. Interestingly, various neurological insults including brain tumors, and neurotropic infections have been shown to have profound impacts on thymic size and cellularity and T cell development. Methods We used flow cytometry, confocal microscopy, neuro tracers, and genetic knockout mice to evaluate developing T cell-neuron interactions within the thymus. Results We have identified neuronal cell bodies within the thymus. These cells express neuronal markers such as NeuN and tyrosine hydroxylase and lack TEC identifiers, such as EpCAM, UEA-1, and Ly51. Thymic neurons can be infected with a neurotropic rabies vector, further cementing their neuronal identity. Considering the thymus consists mostly of T cells, we next investigated a potential interaction between T cells and thymic neurons. Interestingly, loss of T cell development results in a lack of thymic neuron development indicating T cell-neuron coregulation during development. However, thymic neurons can be recovered following a bone marrow transplant, suggesting the provision of a differentiation and/or survival signal for neurons provided by the developing T cells. Using knockout mice lacking the T cell lineage at various stages of development, we determined that TCR beta selection is required for the development of thymic neurons. Finally, we determined that a lack of transition from DN4 to DP severely diminishes the development of thymic neurons. Analysis of the publicly available bulk RNA-sequencing data comparing thymic DP to DN cell populations indicates the potential involvement of IL34 in neuronal development in the thymus. Conclusion We describe a novel population of neuronal cells within the thymus that interact with developing thymocytes to establish optimal thymic architecture. Funding Source NIH K99/R00 Topic Categories Neuroimmunology (NEUR)
View research seven-element analysis
Question and background
The study aimed to investigate the relationship between T cell development and neuronal cell bodies within the thymus, given that neurological insults can impact thymic size and T cell development.
Design and population
The research used flow cytometry, confocal microscopy, neuro tracers, and genetic knockout mice to evaluate T cell-neuron interactions within the thymus.
Core methods
Flow cytometry, confocal microscopy, neuro tracers, and genetic knockout mice were employed to analyze T cell-neuron interactions, and bulk RNA-sequencing data was used to compare thymic DP to DN cell populations.
Main results
Neuronal cell bodies expressing NeuN and tyrosine hydroxylase were identified within the thymus, and their development was found to be dependent on TCR beta selection and the transition from DN4 to DP thymocytes.
Interpretation and novelty
The authors interpreted that developing thymocytes regulate thymic neuron development, establishing a novel population of neuronal cells within the thymus that interact to optimize thymic architecture.
Scientific and public-health significance
The findings provide insights into neuroimmune interactions in the thymus, potentially informing understanding of how neurological conditions affect immune development and offering avenues for further research into thymic-related disorders.
Limitations and evidence strength
The evidence is derived from abstract-only data, and while the methods are robust, the lack of full-text details may limit the assessment of generalizability and potential biases.

口头 狂犬病 疫苗接种 运动 - 俄亥俄州卫生部 (.gov)

Oral Rabies Vaccination Campaign - Ohio Department of Health (.gov)
新闻简报俄亥俄州卫生部与美国农业部野生动物服务部门合作,将于 2026 年 8 月 24 日在俄亥俄州东北部开展口服 狂犬病 狂犬病 运动,对野生浣熊进行 狂犬病 免疫。该活动使用 ONRAB® 疫苗 诱饵在农村地区通过飞机分发,在城市/郊区通过直升机/车辆分发,旨在防止浣熊 狂犬病 的传播,浣熊 狂犬病 于 1996 年引入该地区。虽然 疫苗 对人类和宠物来说是安全的,但建议公众正确处理诱饵,如果宠物误食诱饵,请联系兽医。这一举措是保护俄亥俄州及其家畜免受 狂犬病 侵害的长期努力的一部分。

时间
发布日期:2026年7月28日。活动日期:2026年8月24日(视天气情况而定)。

俄亥俄州东北部,特别是阿什塔比拉、贝尔蒙特、卡罗尔、哥伦比亚纳、哈里森、杰斐逊、马洪宁和特朗布尔县,包括阿什塔比拉、贝尔维斯塔/奥斯汀敦、博德曼、康尼奥特、日内瓦、哈伯德、里斯本、奈尔斯、波兰、斯特拉瑟斯、沃伦和扬斯敦等城市。目标人群:野生浣熊。

在农村地区通过飞机分发诱饵,在城市/郊区通过直升机/车辆分发诱饵。没有报告与 疫苗 直接相关的人类或动物中的 狂犬病 病例。宠物或人类遇到诱饵的风险是众所周知的,但 疫苗 对人类无害,如果大量摄入,只会暂时导致宠物呕吐或腹泻。

A high priorityPublic-health news

Oral Rabies Vaccination Campaign - Ohio Department of Health (.gov)

News Brief
The Ohio Department of Health, in collaboration with the USDA Wildlife Services, is conducting an oral rabies vaccination campaign in northeastern Ohio on August 24, 2026, to immunize wild raccoons against rabies. Using ONRAB® vaccine baits distributed by aircraft in rural areas and by helicopter/vehicle in urban/suburban regions, the campaign aims to prevent the spread of raccoon rabies, which was introduced to the area in 1996. While the vaccine is safe for humans and pets, the public is advised on proper bait handling and to contact veterinarians if pets consume baits. This initiative is part of a long-term effort to protect Ohioans and their domestic animals from rabies.
Original source text (English)

Close Ohio Department of Health Menu Home Who We Are About Us Information & Programs Health Rules Laws & Forms Explore Data & Stats Find Local Health Departments Media Center Help Center Search A➝Z index abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz× Help Go to the help page Search Search in our portal Type in your search keywords and hit enter to submit or escape to close Search Close Oral Rabies Vaccination Campaign {} Web Content Viewer Actions ODH Information & Programs Zoonotic Disease Program News Oral Rabies Vaccination Campaign Welcome News About Us Animals Diseases Prevention Materials Statistics and Maps Newsletter Videos Laws & Rules Media Rabies Post-Exposure Risk Assessment Tool Expired Content Oral Rabies Vaccination Campaign July 28, 2026 Baits are dropped in northeastern Ohio to immunize wild raccoons for rabies. In 1996, a new strain of rabies in wild raccoons was introduced into northeastern Ohio from Pennsylvania. To protect Ohioans and their domestic animals, the Ohio Department of Health (ODH) and other state and local agencies partnered with the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA) Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service Wildlife Services to implement a program to immunize wild raccoons for rabies using an oral rabies vaccine (ORV). This effort created a barrier of immune animals that reduced animal cases and prevented the spread of raccoon rabies into the rest of Ohio. The vaccine-laden baits are dropped by fixed wing aircraft in rural areas and by low-flying helicopters and ground vehicles in urban and suburban neighborhoods. If you have found a bait or have questions, call the Ohio Department of Health Rabies Information Line at (888) 574-6656. Although placement is targeted to raccoon habitat, it is inevitable that some baits may end up in a yard or be found by a pet or person. Dogs, in particular, are attracted to them. Please refer to the information below if you or your pet finds a bait. Expand All Sections Where does baiting occur? The 2026 fall operation will take place Aug. 7 through Aug. 24, 2026, contingent on weather. Baiting will take place in Ashtabula, Belmont, Carroll, Columbiana, Harrison, Jefferson, Mahoning, and Trumbull counties, including the cities of Ashtabula, Belle Vista/Austintown, Boardman, Conneaut, Geneva, Hubbard, Lisbon, Niles, Poland, Struthers, Warren, and Youngstown. For updates and access to near real-time data for the national baiting operation, please visit the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA)'s Daily Status dashboard. One type of bait will be used for the 2026 fall operation: The ONRAB® vaccine is enclosed in a 1" x 2" blister pack filled with the vaccine and covered with a sweet-smelling dark green waxy coating. The baits contain a rabies vaccine that if consumed by a raccoon should vaccinate the animal against the rabies virus. ONRAB® does not contain rabies virus but contains a gene from the rabies virus that causes raccoons and skunks that come in contact with the vaccine to produce antibodies that protect them against rabies infection. Ohio is one of seven states using ONRAB® for baiting operations in the U.S. What if my pet eats the bait? click to expand Ingesting the vaccine will not harm your pet, although eating a large number may temporarily cause vomiting or diarrhea. It is recommended to contact a veterinarian as a precaution if a pet has consumed the vaccine baits. Do not attempt to remove a bait from your pet's mouth – doing so may put you at risk of being bitten. Confine your pet for a couple of days and check the area for more baits. If you find a bait, leave it undisturbed where you found it, unless found in your lawn, driveway, or other area where your pet may find it. Baits can be moved, using gloves, to another location that is less accessible to people or pets where raccoons are more likely to find it. Most baits are gone within four days. Try to avoid your pet's saliva for 24 hours, and wash skin or wounds that may have been licked. Vaccination of your pets remains critical to prevent rabies. The oral rabies vaccine bait is intended for oral vaccination of wildlife only. Consult with a veterinarian about vaccination options for your pets. What if I find a rabies bait? click to expand Baits should be left undisturbed, but intact baits can be moved if they are found in your lawn, driveway, or other area where children and pets may find it. Damaged baits should be bagged and disposed in the trash. Wear gloves or use a paper towel when picking up the bait. Toss intact baits into a fence row, woodlot, ditch, or other raccoon habitat area. Wash your hands well with soap and water after any skin contact with damaged baits. Contact with intact baits is not harmful. Persons who are immunocompromised or pregnant should contact a physician if the bait ruptures and vaccine gets into a mucous membrane or open wound. If a person is exposed to the vaccine (liquid) within the bait, thoroughly wash any areas of the exposed skin with soap and warm water. How are the baits distributed? click to expand Working with employees from cooperating agencies, USDA Wildlife Services distributes baits in urban area and suburban areas by vehicle or helicopter. In rural and open areas, the baits are distributed by aircraft. Can I get rabies from contact with the vaccine? click to expand No. The vaccines do not contain the live rabies virus but contains a gene from the rabies virus that causes raccoons and skunks that come in contact with the vaccine to produce antibodies that protect them against rabies infection. How does a raccoon, skunk, fox, or coyote get vaccinated by eating the ORV bait? click to expand The vaccine is contained inside a blister packet, which is made attractive to wildlife with a sweet attractant. When an animal bites into the bait, it punctures the blister pack and the vaccine bathes the oral cavity and tonsils, resulting in an oral vaccination against rabies. The animal's immune system is exposed to the part of the rabies virus that causes an immune response and production of antibodies against rabies, but the vaccine cannot cause rabies. The blueprint for making antibodies to neutralize the rabies virus is stored in the animal's immune system, allowing it to respond quickly if it is later exposed to a rabid animal. Publication date: July 28, 2026. Event date: August 24, 2026 (contingent on weather).
Location and population
Northeastern Ohio, specifically Ashtabula, Belmont, Carroll, Columbiana, Harrison, Jefferson, Mahoning, and Trumbull counties, including cities such as Ashtabula, Belle Vista/Austintown, Boardman, Conneaut, Geneva, Hubbard, Lisbon, Niles, Poland, Struthers, Warren, and Youngstown. Target population: Wild raccoons.
Event
Confirmed oral rabies vaccination campaign conducted by the Ohio Department of Health (ODH) in partnership with the USDA Wildlife Services to immunize wild raccoons against rabies using ONRAB® vaccine baits.
Scale, impact and risk
Baits distributed by aircraft in rural areas and by helicopter/vehicle in urban/suburban areas. No cases of rabies reported in humans or animals directly linked to the vaccine. Risk of pets or humans encountering baits is acknowledged, but the vaccine is not harmful to humans and only temporarily may cause vomiting or diarrhea in pets if ingested in large quantities.
Response, status and uncertainty
Campaign is part of an ongoing program since 1996 to prevent the spread of raccoon rabies. Public guidance provided for handling baits and contacting veterinarians if pets consume baits. Uncertainty exists regarding weather dependency for the August 24, 2026 operation.

DOH-Marion Issues Rabies Advisory - marion.floridahealth.gov

News Brief
The Florida Department of Health in Marion County has issued a rabies advisory following a single confirmed case of rabies in a raccoon. The health department is actively monitoring rabies activity among wild animals in the area. Residents are reminded to be cautious around wildlife and report any suspicious animal behavior. The advisory underscores the importance of rabies prevention and awareness in the community. No human cases have been reported at this time.

A high priorityPublic-health news

DOH-Pasco Issues Rabies Advisory - Florida Department of Health in Pasco County (.gov)

News Brief
The Florida Department of Health in Pasco County has issued a rabies advisory following the confirmation of two rabies cases in raccoons on the east side of the county. The advisory serves as a warning to residents about potential rabies exposure in the area. While no human cases have been reported, the health department is urging caution and reminding the public to avoid contact with wild animals. The advisory highlights the importance of rabies prevention measures, including vaccinating pets and seeking medical attention if bitten or scratched by an animal. The health department is continuing to monitor the situation and will provide updates as needed.

切勿触摸蝙蝠:关于蝙蝠和狂犬病,你需要知道些什么 - Public Health Insider

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)
新闻简报公共卫生——西雅图和金县在伦顿(7 月 29 日)和西雅图(7 月 15 日)发现狂犬病蝙蝠后发出警告。伦顿蝙蝠是在吉恩库隆公园发现的,而西雅图蝙蝠是在华盛顿大学校园附近发现的。当局强调,任何与这些蝙蝠有身体接触的人或动物,即使没有被咬,也可能面临 狂犬病 的风险,应立即寻求医疗评估。建议公众不要触摸蝙蝠,尤其是那些表现异常的蝙蝠,例如躺在地上、白天飞行或发出过大噪音的蝙蝠。对于西雅图事件,敦促暴露者致电 206-296-4774 联系公共卫生部门,以评估 狂犬病 预防性 治疗 的需求。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026年7月30日。活动日期:2026年7月29日(伦顿蝙蝠)和2026年7月15日(西雅图蝙蝠)。
地点与对象
美国华盛顿州金县。具体地点:Renton 的 Gene Coulon Park 和西雅图靠近 University Way Northeast 的华盛顿大学校园。
事件
在伦顿和西雅图发现确诊的狂犬病蝙蝠。公共卫生——西雅图和金县报告了这些事件,并警告潜在的 狂犬病 暴露风险。
规模、影响与风险
没有提及具体案例数。风险适用于任何与蝙蝠有身体接触的人,即使没有被蝙蝠咬伤。预防性 治疗 可能是必要的。
应对、状态与不确定性
公共卫生部门建议立即对接触者进行医疗评估,并提供联系信息 (206-296-4774),以确定是否需要 狂犬病 预防性 治疗。来源是来自知名公共卫生机构的完整原始报告。

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)

Original title (Chinese)
切勿触摸蝙蝠:关于蝙蝠和狂犬病,你需要知道些什么 - Public Health Insider
News Brief
Public Health – Seattle & King County has issued warnings after rabid bats were found in Renton (July 29) and Seattle (July 15). The Renton bat was discovered at Gene Coulon Park, while the Seattle bat was found near the University of Washington campus. Authorities emphasize that any person or animal with physical contact with these bats, even without a bite, could be at risk for rabies and should seek immediate medical evaluation. The public is advised not to touch bats, especially those acting abnormally, such as lying on the ground, flying during daytime, or making excessive noise. For the Seattle incident, exposed individuals are urged to call Public Health at 206-296-4774 to assess the need for rabies preventative treatment.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

野生动物 疫苗 计划于 8 月投放,以帮助减缓 狂犬病 的传播 - 佛蒙特州商业杂志

Wildlife vaccine drop planned for August to help slow spread of rabies - Vermont Business Magazine
新闻简报佛蒙特州将于 2026 年 8 月 1 日开始年度 狂犬病 疫苗 诱饵投放,以应对野生动物中不断上升的 狂犬病 率。农业部野生动物服务部将与佛蒙特州卫生和鱼类与野生动物部门协调,向佛蒙特州 10 个县以及纽约州和新罕布什尔州部分地区分发约 915,700 个 疫苗 诱饵。狂犬病 活动激增,2024 年和 2025 年报告了 66 例病例,是前十年平均水平的两倍多,2026 年已报告 36 例。几乎每个佛蒙特州县都受到影响,其中奥尔良县集中度较高。对浣熊和臭鼬具有吸引力的口服疫苗诱饵将在农村地区通过飞机、在郊区通过直升机、在居民区通过手工投放。官员们继续了解增加情况,同时敦促公众通过佛蒙特州 狂犬病 热线报告疑虑,并确保宠物接种疫苗。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表日期:2026-07-28。事件日期:2026年8月1日(疫苗诱饵投放开始)。
地点与对象
美国佛蒙特州(县:艾迪生、喀里多尼亚、奇滕登、埃塞克斯、富兰克林、格兰德艾尔、拉莫伊尔、奥兰治、奥尔良、华盛顿),以及纽约州和新罕布什尔州的部分地区。受影响人群:野生动物(浣熊、臭鼬、狐狸、蝙蝠、土拨鼠)和面临风险的人类/宠物。
事件
确认年度 狂犬病 疫苗 诱饵投放,以应对佛蒙特州野生动物不断上升的 狂犬病 率。由农业部野生动物服务部与佛蒙特州卫生和鱼类与野生动物部门协调开展。
规模、影响与风险
大约分发 915,700 个 狂犬病 疫苗 诱饵。狂犬病 活动急剧增加,2024 年和 2025 年报告了 66 只患有狂犬病的动物(是前 10 年平均水平的两倍多)。2026 年迄今为止,已报告 36 起 狂犬病 病例(15 只浣熊、9 只臭鼬、8 只蝙蝠、3 只狐狸)。佛蒙特州几乎每个县都受到影响,其中许多病例集中在奥尔良县。狂犬病 是一种致命的病毒性疾病,主要通过动物叮咬传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗接种 正在努力通过飞机、直升机和人工分发诱饵。官员们正在努力了解 狂犬病 病例的增加情况,狂犬病 的工作仍在继续。建议公众联系佛蒙特州 狂犬病 热线 (1-800-4-狂犬病) 咨询疑虑。宠物和牲畜应及时更新 疫苗接种。来源限制:已审查完整的原始文章。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Wildlife vaccine drop planned for August to help slow spread of rabies - Vermont Business Magazine

News Brief
Vermont will begin its annual rabies vaccine bait drop on August 1, 2026, to combat rising rabies rates in wildlife. The Department of Agriculture’s Wildlife Services, in coordination with the Vermont departments of Health and Fish & Wildlife, will distribute approximately 915,700 vaccine baits across 10 Vermont counties, as well as parts of New York and New Hampshire. Rabies activity has surged, with 66 cases reported in both 2024 and 2025—more than double the previous decade's average—and 36 cases already in 2026. Nearly every Vermont county has been affected, with Orleans County seeing high concentrations. The oral vaccine baits, attractive to raccoons and skunks, will be deployed by airplane in rural areas, helicopter in suburban areas, and by hand in residential areas. Officials continue surveillance to understand the increase, while the public is urged to report concerns via the Vermont Rabies Hotline and ensure pets are vaccinated.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main navigation Login Logout SUBSCRIBE Sign up for FREE daily enews × Menu Magazine Profiles in Business Giving Guide Digital Archives News People In The News Company Database Contact Us Advertise All Events Special Events -A +A Wildlife vaccine drop planned for August to help slow spread of rabies Opens in a new window Opens in a new window Opens in a new window Submitted by tim on Tue, 07/28/2026 - 05:17 Vermont Business Magazine The annual rabies vaccine bait drop will begin on Aug. 1 to continue combating rising rabies rates in Vermont wildlife. The U.S. Department of Agriculture’s Wildlife Services, in coordination with the Vermont departments of Health and Fish & Wildlife, will conduct the drops. The August rabies bait distribution will deliver approximately 915,700 rabies vaccine baits in areas of Addison, Caledonia, Chittenden, Essex, Franklin, Grand Isle, Lamoille, Orange, Orleans and Washington Counties, as well as parts of New York and New Hampshire. “Vaccinating wildlife against rabies remains an important tool to help protect people and animals, and slow the spread of rabies in Vermont,” said Natalie Kwit, DVM, state public health veterinarian with the Health Department. Rabies activity has risen sharply in recent years, with 66 rabid animals reported in both 2024 and 2025 – more than double the previous 10-year annual average number of cases. So far this year, 36 rabies cases have been reported – including 15 raccoons, nine skunks, eight bats and three foxes. Nearly every county in Vermont has been affected, with many cases concentrated in Orleans County. Officials are working to understand why more animals are testing positive for rabies, and surveillance efforts to monitor the increase are ongoing. Over the next month, baited vaccine – in the form of a sweet-smelling oral bait that is attractive for raccoons and skunks to eat – will be distributed across 10 counties by airplane in rural areas, helicopter in suburban areas, and by hand in residential areas. Find more information, including a full list of towns where bait will be distributed, on the Rabies Vaccine Bait Drop Fact Sheet on the Health Department’s website. Rabies is a viral disease that is deadly if people don't receive medical care before symptoms start. It is primarily spread through the bite of an infected animal. In Vermont, the disease is most often detected in raccoons, skunks, foxes, bats and woodchucks – but pets and livestock can also get rabies if they have not been vaccinated. Human rabies cases in the U.S. are most commonly caused by contact with bats. How to protect yourself and your pets: If you are bitten by an animal, wash the wound well with soap and water and contact your health care provider. Do not touch or handle wild or stray animals, even young ones. Learn more about what to do when you encounter a wild animal from the Department of Fish and Wildlife. Call the Vermont Rabies Hotline (1-800-4-RABIES) if you see a sick or strangely behaving animal, are bitten by a wild animal, or wake up to a bat in your sleeping area. Contact local law enforcement regarding concerns about stray pets. Make sure pets and livestock are up to date on vaccination. Keep pets indoors at night and supervise them when outside. During the rabies bait drop, leave vaccine baits where they are and call the Rabies Hotline if a pet or child eats or brings one home. Learn more about rabies in Vermont – visit www.HealthVermont.gov/rabies. About the Department of Health We have been the state's public health agency for 140 years, working every day to protect and promote the health of Vermonters. Time
Publication date: 2026-07-28. Event date: August 1, 2026 (start of vaccine bait drop).
Location and population
Vermont, USA (counties: Addison, Caledonia, Chittenden, Essex, Franklin, Grand Isle, Lamoille, Orange, Orleans, Washington), with parts of New York and New Hampshire. Affected population: wildlife (raccoons, skunks, foxes, bats, woodchucks) and humans/pets at risk.
Event
Confirmed annual rabies vaccine bait drop to combat rising rabies rates in Vermont wildlife. Conducted by the Department of Agriculture’s Wildlife Services, in coordination with Vermont departments of Health and Fish & Wildlife.
Scale, impact and risk
Approximately 915,700 rabies vaccine baits to be distributed. Rabies activity has risen sharply, with 66 rabid animals reported in both 2024 and 2025 (more than double the previous 10-year average). So far in 2026, 36 rabies cases have been reported (15 raccoons, nine skunks, eight bats, three foxes). Nearly every county in Vermont has been affected, with many cases concentrated in Orleans County. Rabies is a deadly viral disease primarily spread through animal bites.
Response, status and uncertainty
Vaccination efforts ongoing with bait distribution by airplane, helicopter, and hand. Officials are working to understand the increase in rabies cases, with surveillance efforts continuing. Public advised to contact the Vermont Rabies Hotline (1-800-4-RABIES) for concerns. Pets and livestock should be up to date on vaccination. Source limitation: Full original article reviewed.

今年夏天第二只蝙蝠检测出 狂犬病 呈阳性

Second bat tests positive for rabies this summer
新闻简报迪卡尔布县动物控制中心已确认今年夏天第二例蝙蝠感染 狂犬病 病例,7 月 21 日,哥伦比亚大道和约克广场附近的一只死蝙蝠检测结果呈阳性。此前,7 月初在 Stone Mountain 的 Millstone Trail 附近也曾出现过一例病例。在美国,蝙蝠被认为是 狂犬病 的主要来源,它们的细小牙齿可能会导致咬伤或抓伤,而不会被注意到。官员们强调,狂犬病 是一种通过唾液(通常是叮咬)传播的脑部疾病,并警告说,一旦出现临床症状,就不存在 治疗。敦促居民避免接触蝙蝠,确保宠物接种疫苗,并消除户外食物来源以降低风险。如果在症状出现之前施用,暴露后 疫苗 是有效的。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Second bat tests positive for rabies this summer

News Brief
DeKalb County Animal Control has confirmed a second case of rabies in a bat this summer, with a dead bat testing positive near Columbia Drive and York Place on July 21. This follows an earlier case in early July near Stone Mountain's Millstone Trail. Bats are identified as the leading source of rabies in the U.S., with their tiny teeth making bites or scratches potentially unnoticed. Officials emphasize that rabies is a brain disease spread through saliva, usually via bites, and warn that once clinical signs appear, there is no treatment. Residents are urged to avoid touching bats, ensure pets are vaccinated, and eliminate outdoor food sources to reduce risk. Post-exposure vaccines are effective if administered before symptoms develop.
Original source text (English)
Skip to content Advertise News Business Opinion Education Lifestyle Sports Cities Avondale Estates Brookhaven Chamblee Clarkston Countywide Decatur Doraville Dunwoody Ellenwood Lithonia Pine Lake Stone Mountain Stonecrest Tucker Contact Us Legal Advertising Dept LOCATIONS Subscribe News Second bat tests positive for rabies this summer July 27, 2026 Christine Fonville A second case of rabies this summer in a bat has been confirmed by DeKalb County Animal Control (DCAES). On July 21, officials stated that a dead bat tested positive for rabies in south DeKalb near Columbia Drive and York Place. Early in July, a bat found near a neighborhood in Stone Mountain also tested positive for rabies after being found dead near Millstone Trail. While multiple mammals can carry and contract rabies, officials with Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) state that bats are the leading source of rabies in the U.S., and their teeth are so tiny that a bite or scratch can go unnoticed. “Avoid touching bats and seek a medical professional if you may have come into contact with one,” the CDC’s website states. “Rabies is a disease that affects the brain, and it is usually passed from animal to animal, but can be passed from animals to people,” said officials with DCAES. “The virus is spread through saliva, usually from a bite of an animal that has the disease.” According to the American Veterinary Medical Association, “Animals with rabies may show a variety of signs, including fearfulness, aggression, excessive drooling, difficulty swallowing, staggering, paralysis, and seizures. Aggressive behavior is common, but rabid animals may also be uncharacteristically affectionate. Rabid wild animals may lose their natural fear of humans and display unusual behavior; for example, an animal that is usually only seen at night may be seen wandering in the daytime.” There is no treatment once the clinical signs of rabies appear, stated the American Veterinary Medical Association’s website. People who are exposed to rabies through an animal exposure can be treated with “vaccines that provide immunity to rabies when administered soon after an exposure,” according to the Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine. The vaccines must be administered before symptoms of the disease are present to be effective, stated officials. “Georgia is a rabies endemic state, and the virus is present all year long,” said DCAES officials. “It is not unusual for the county to report several rabies cases each year.” Residents should make sure pets are up to date for preventative vaccination through their veterinarian and should eliminate outdoor feeding stations and access to garbage cans, said officials. Additionally, residents are advised to watch pets and contact DeKalb County Animal Control if unusual behavior occurs. “Vaccinations are effective in protecting humans and pets from the virus,” stated officials. “Residents are responsible for vaccinating pets every year and registering the tag with DeKalb County Animal Control.” For more information or to report any animal bite, contact DeKalb County Animal Control at 404-294-2996, from 9 a.m. to 5 p.m., Monday through Friday, or 404-294-2519 after hours. This latest case in south DeKalb marks the fourth time this year that DCAES officials have announced an animal was found to test positive for rabies. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Oregon matches record for bats testing positive with rabies

News Brief
Oregon has matched its record for bats testing positive with rabies in 2026, with 22 cases confirmed by the Oregon Health Authority and the Oregon Department of Fish and Wildlife. Bats are the primary animal to expose humans to rabies in the U.S., and officials are urging residents to avoid contact with dead or dying bats to prevent transmission. The virus can spread through bites or scratches from infected bats, posing a risk to both humans and pets. Public health guidelines include reporting exposures, seeking immediate medical care, and ensuring pets are vaccinated. Unvaccinated pets exposed to bats may face quarantine or euthanasia. Authorities emphasize the importance of wildlife protection while minimizing rabies risks.
Original source text (English)

After 22 Oregon bats have tested positive for rabies in 2026, Oregon Health Authority and the Oregon Department of Fish and Wildlife are reminding people how to prevent exposure risk. The primary way people can stay safe is to not touch dead or dying animals, officials said. Also, to keep pets away. Oregon has matched its record for bats that tested positive for rabies in a year, OHA said. Bats are the primary animal to expose humans to the virus in the U.S. Bats rank second behind skunks in animals that have tested positive for rabies in Larimer County in recent years. 636525557169345144 Bat Though people might want to help a dead or dying bat, more contact with bats increased rabies exposure risk, OHA's Public Health Division veterinarian Emilio DeBess said. Advertisement Advertisement "When a bat or other wild animal is sick or dying with rabies, there's an increased chance they will bite or transmit the virus in other ways," DeBess said. Bats and other wildlife rarely bite people, but might if they are sick or threatened, ODFW state wildlife veterinarian Colin Gillin said. "Bats provide important ecosystem services in Oregon, but also globally with insect control, seed dispersal, and pollination of specific plants, supporting agriculture and forest systems," Gillin said. "And like many animals that can carry disease, bats can also prevent or suppress disease by reducing mosquitoes and other vectors that carry human and animal pathogens." What to do if you come across a bat Since bats are protected wildlife, and it is illegal to harm or keep them. ODFW and OHA provided the following list of tips if you see a sick bat: Advertisement Advertisement Stay calm, do not touch it. Keep people and pets away. If the sick or dead bat is indoors, do not release it. It may have exposed someone or a pet to rabies. Place a container or box over the bat if it's safe. If the sick or dead bat is outdoors and may have exposed someone to rabies, cover it with a box or bucket to keep it in place. But, leave the bat alone unless someone was bitten or scratched before it died. Immediately report if a person or pet has been bitten or scratched by an animal that may have rabies to local animal regulation services or local public health authority. Seek medical care immediately from a medical provider or veterinarian. If multiple sick or dead bats are observed in an area report to ODFW. Avoid bats seen in the wild, such as while hiking. Teach children to avoid all contact with bats and other wildlife. Update dog or cat vaccinations, whether they are indoor or outdoor pets. Unvaccinated pets that come into contact with a bat may be quarantined for up to four months, or euthanized. Protect your home from bats by covering vents, chimneys, and other entry points with screens. If roosting bats are living in the attic, call a wildlife control operator, not ODFW. More information about rabies and bats is avaliable at OHA's bats and rabies website and ODFW's bats website. Makenzie Stamets is an outdoor intern at the Statesman Journal. She can be reached at mstamets@statesmanjournal.com or on X at @MakenzieStamets. 狂犬病 疫苗 空投在圣劳伦斯县进行,另一只浣熊测试呈阳性 - nny360.com

Rabies vaccine airdrop takes place in St. Lawrence County as another raccoon tests positive - nny360.com
新闻简报在纽约州圣劳伦斯县,当局已确认浣熊感染 狂犬病 病例,促使美国农业部野生动物管理局本周进行了空中 疫苗 诱饵分发活动。这一举措是更广泛的全国合作努力的一部分,旨在预防 狂犬病 传播,这是一种对动物和人类致命的疾病。疫苗 的目标投放旨在对野生动物群体(尤其是浣熊)进行免疫,以遏制病毒的传播。该行动强调了正在采取的公共卫生措施,以管理野生动物与人类互动常见的农村和郊区的 狂犬病 风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 31 日;事件发生在 2026 年 8 月 1 日之前的一周内。
地点与对象
劳伦斯县,纽约州,美国;将浣熊种群作为野生动物 狂犬病 预防的一部分。
事件
浣熊确诊 狂犬病 病例;美国农业部野生动物服务部门在圣路易斯进行了空中 狂犬病 疫苗 诱饵分发活动。
规模、影响与风险
浣熊狂犬病确诊病例1例;狂犬病 传播给动物和人类的风险,如果不及时治疗,将致命。
应对、状态与不确定性
美国农业部野生动物服务部门通过空中分发 疫苗 积极响应;全国合作 狂犬病 预防工作的一部分;没有报告未解决的细节。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Rabies vaccine airdrop takes place in St. Lawrence County as another raccoon tests positive - nny360.com

News Brief
In St. Lawrence County, New York, authorities have confirmed a case of rabies in a raccoon, prompting the USDA Wildlife Services to conduct an aerial vaccine bait distribution this week. This initiative is part of a broader, nationwide cooperative effort to prevent rabies transmission, a disease fatal to both animals and humans. The targeted vaccine drop aims to immunize wildlife populations, particularly raccoons, to curb the spread of the virus. The operation underscores ongoing public health measures to manage rabies risks in rural and suburban areas where wildlife-human interactions are common.
Original source text (English)

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Merced County Sheriff's Animal Services Bureau Offer Safety Tips to Prevent Rabies Exposure and Keep Family and Pets Safe - Sierra Sun Times

News Brief
Merced County Sheriff's Animal Services Bureau has issued safety tips to prevent rabies exposure and protect families and pets. Officials highlight that while human rabies cases are rare, the disease is almost always fatal once symptoms appear, emphasizing the importance of prompt medical evaluation after potential exposure. Rabies is primarily transmitted through the saliva of infected animals via bites. The bureau advises the public to avoid contact with bats, keep children and pets away from them, and seek professional assistance if a bat is found indoors. California mandates rabies vaccinations for dogs and strongly recommends them for cats, as keeping pets current on vaccinations is the best protection against wildlife encounters. Simple precautions can help ensure safety while preserving bats' ecological role.
Original source text (English)
× Web Image Sort by Relevance Date Home News Local News Booking Report Letters to the Editor Obituaries California U.S. & World Politics Advertising Specials Senior News Fire Call Log Fire News Advertise Contact Us Discover more Vehicle & Traffic Safety pet Demographics Merced County Sheriff's Animal Services Bureau Offer Safety Tips to Prevent Rabies Exposure and Keep Family and Pets Safe Details Last Updated: Wednesday, 29 July 2026 03:40 Published: Wednesday, 29 July 2026 05:02 Rabies Awareness: Avoid Contact with Bats July 29, 2026 – Merced County Sheriff's Animal Services Bureau officials remind the public about the dangers of rabies. Bats are an important part of our ecosystem, but some can carry rabies. Rabies is spread through the saliva of an infected animal, usually through a bite. While human rabies cases are rare, the disease is almost always fatal once symptoms develop. Prompt medical evaluation after a potential exposure can prevent rabies from developing. If you encounter a bat: - Do not touch or handle it, even if it appears sick, injured, or unable to fly. - Keep children and pets away. - If a bat is found inside your home, close the door to the room if possible and contact a licensed wildlife exclusion or pest control professional for assistance. - If you, a family member, or your pet may have had contact with a bat—even if you are unsure whether a bite occurred—contact your healthcare provider, veterinarian, or local Animal Services as soon as possible. Bat bites can be small and difficult to detect. Discover more Occupational Health & Safety Wildlife News California law requires dogs to be vaccinated against rabies, and rabies vaccination is strongly recommended for cats. Keeping pets current on their rabies vaccinations is the best way to protect them if they come into contact with wildlife. Simple precautions can help protect you, your family, and your pets while allowing bats to continue their important role in our environment. B routine priorityPublic-health news

2nd raccoon sought after rabies-positive animal attacks Glassboro, NJ man - 6abc Philadelphia

News Brief
A Glassboro, New Jersey man was attacked by a rabies-positive raccoon on Sunday morning, resulting in a bite to his leg. The raccoon was captured and tested positive for rabies, and authorities are now searching for a second raccoon that had been seen fighting with the infected animal. The victim, Kevin Tucker, is undergoing rabies treatment and has warned neighbors. The Gloucester County Health Department has advised residents to avoid wild animals and keep pets vaccinated. The incident highlights ongoing rabies risks in the area.
Original source text (English)
24/7 LivePhiladelphiaPennsylvaniaNew JerseyDelaware 89° Raccoons 2nd raccoon sought after rabies-positive animal attacks South Jersey man By6abc Digital Staff and Caroline Goggin Thursday, July 30, 2026 3:35AM 2nd raccoon sought after rabies-positive animal attacks NJ man GLASSBORO, N.J. (WPVI) -- Gloucester County officials are searching for a raccoon after another animal involved in an encounter tested positive for rabies. The investigation stems from an attack Sunday morning outside the Glassboro home of Kevin Tucker. "I was walking to leave when a raccoon came out of the shrubbery and charged me," Tucker said. "I kind of fell backward, and I think that's when it bit the front part of my right leg." READ MORE | Man recalls raccoon attack outside NJ home: 'I could see these bright yellow eyes' The raccoon was eventually captured in Anthony Alvarado's backyard - just steps from where Tucker was bitten. "It was running around making weird noises," Alvarado told Action News. "It was actually kind of foaming at the mouth a bit, so that's how I knew it had rabies." Officials said the raccoon that attacked Tucker has since tested positive for rabies. Authorities also said the infected animal had been seen fighting with another raccoon before its capture. Efforts are now underway to locate that second raccoon. Action News returned to the neighborhood on Wednesday afternoon to speak with Tucker, who said he is now on high alert. "I just don't know what might pop out of where and when," he said. He has spent the past few days fielding phone calls from the Gloucester County Health Department and Gloucester County Animal Control, as he also undergoes a series of rabies shots. He had his second round of shots on Wednesday. Tucker has also spent time warning his neighbors about the situation. Christina Gulla, who lives in the neighborhood, said, "It's a little scary. I have kids. Gotta keep them inside, I guess." Although the shots are unpleasant, Tucker said he is thankful he is getting treated. He told Action News he will continue to keep his guard up as officials look for the second raccoon. "You live in nature, so you just gotta be aware of nature," Tucker said. "But, as I said, I was in my yard; you're not expecting something to come shooting out of the bushes." The Gloucester County Health Department is warning residents to avoid contact with wild animals and to keep pets up to date on their vaccinations. Anyone bitten or scratched by a wild animal should seek medical attention immediately and contact local animal control or health officials. Report a correction or typo Copyright © 2026 WPVI-TV. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Several Hunter Creek residents get rabies vaccines after bat exposures - Aspen Daily News

News Brief
In Hunter Creek, Colorado, a bat infestation has led to six residents being recommended for post-exposure rabies vaccines, though no bats have tested positive for rabies. The infestation, centered in one condominium unit, has displaced two residents and prompted a building-wide risk assessment. County officials are working with pest control companies to remove the bats, using an agitator to force them out before sealing entry points. The Romero Group is covering all medical and repair costs. Officials emphasize that while bats may appear lethargic, they are likely juvenile bats learning to fly, not sick. Residents are advised to keep doors and windows closed and pets on leash to prevent further exposure.
Original source text (English)
Saturday, August 187°F / Aspen, CO e-Edition News News Latest News Government Cops and Courts Arts & Entertainment Sports Business Business Business News Real Estate Transactions Opinion Letters to Editor A&E Calendar Calendar Calendar of Events Submit a Calendar Event Obituaries About Shop Donate e-Edition Main e-Edition NewsBusiness Opinion & Commentary Letters to Editor Arts & Entertainment Calendar Local Magazine Obituaries About Newsletters Classifieds Shop Donate About Contact Us Impact Report Ethics Policy Advertise ADN Story Studio Media Kit Social Instagram Facebook X News July 31, 2026 Several Hunter Creek residents get rabies vaccines after bat exposures No bats have tested positive, but officials recommend shots as preventive measure Lucy PetersonAspen Daily News Staff Writer Pitkin County officials are working to mitigate a bat infestation in Hunter Creek, capturing and testing some for rabies. No bats have tested positive, but some residents were required to get post-exposure rabies vaccinations. Courtesy of Lauren Wenzel-Martenson/Snowmass Village Animal Services Pitkin County officials are mitigating a bat infestation in Hunter Creek that has required several people to get post-exposure rabies vaccinations and displaced two residents. One unit in the Hunter Creek Condominiums had “concerning bat activity,” said Carlyn Porter, Pitkin County’s emergency response and epidemiology program administrator, after a hole in the roof had previously been sealed and trapped a bat colony inside. Six Hunter Creek residents have been recommended for a post-exposure prophylaxis rabies vaccine, according to a letter sent to residents by The Romero Group, the property manager. It is a series of shots over a few weeks after exposure. “Two different pest control companies are working on the process,” Porter said. “We also want to remind folks that it can be challenging to mitigate while the bats are roosting during their breeding season, so right now we’re seeing a lot of baby bats ... that are just learning how to fly, and that’s the main reason the bats are coming into human habitation is to roost and have their babies.” While the county is seeing a “potentially record-breaking year of rabies activity in terms of potential exposures,” Porter said, none of the recovered bats have tested positive for rabies. But, especially in cases of infestations, anyone who has been exposed to bats is recommended to receive the rabies vaccines to prevent infection. “Typically, [if] it’s one bat we can isolate, we can test,” Porter said. “When it’s an entire colony, it’s really a shot in the dark if you’re testing one bat, and there’s potentially 100 more, so we’re making more recommendations [for vaccines] than we would in a situation where it’s an isolated exposure.” Advertisement In Hunter Creek, “it’s been a building-wide risk assessment process where we’re going unit by unit and determining if there was any activity,” Porter said. Susan Capiel, who lives directly below the Hunter Creek unit that had the infestation, captured two bats in her unit and released them. She and her husband are currently receiving their rabies vaccines. They have been keeping their doors and windows closed — despite the heat — in order to avoid another bat encounter. “We’re going to have to put up with this until it gets cooler and the bats migrate,” Capiel said. “I’ve been just still closing the doors over the screens at night because I just don’t want to be sleeping and have something happen.” Keep up or miss out!Wake up to today's headlines in your inbox. Information is just a click away. Do not fill this field Sign Up Capiel and Porter both said the residents of the infested unit are moving out because of the infestation. The Romero Group is working with the county’s health department, two pest control companies and two cleaning companies to address the infestation and make sure the affected unit is habitable, according to the letter sent to residents and reviewed by the Aspen Daily News. The Romero Group will also cover all expenses for physical repairs to HOA property caused by the incident, cleaning and testing costs inside the affected unit and will reimburse any uninsured medical expenses for the six individuals in Hunter Creek who elected to undergo the rabies vaccination, according to the letter. Porter and Aspen Wildlife Coordinator Lara Xaiz said bedroom exposures are the highest risk, since people can’t be sure if a bat has scratched or bitten them in their sleep. They suggest isolating bats to one room by shutting doors and windows and placing a towel under the door so the bat can’t escape, before calling animal control. They also recommend trying to place a box or Tupperware container over a bat that is on the ground. A bat specialist is setting up an agitator in the infested unit to force the bats to leave and not return. They will then seal any entrances after the bats leave. As officials mitigate the bat colony, people may see bats around town or on the ground. While they may look lethargic or sick, Xaiz said they likely are not. Most of them are juvenile bats who struggle to fly when they are on the ground and are just not strong enough to fly yet, she said. “There is no indication that these bats are sick,” Xaiz said. She added that people should keep pets on a leash and not handle the bats themselves. “Never handle, never touch, keep your screens closed so they don’t come flying into your building or your home at night,” Xaiz said. lucy@aspendailynews.com Story Tags Health Advertisement Related News Aug. 1 Aspen Area Community Trust receives nonprofit status News Aug. 1 Cheerleading for democracy, McFaul draws packed house News Aug. 1 Local news in brief, Aug. 1 News July 31 Glenwood Canyon construction planned during 2027 airport clo... B routine priorityPublic-health news

Teenager swimming in Maryland lake bitten by rabid beaver - WTOP

News Brief
A teenager swimming in a Maryland lake was bitten by a beaver that later tested positive for rabies, prompting health officials to close a section of Cunningham Falls State Park. The incident occurred Sunday morning in a designated swim area at Hunting Creek Lake. Animal control retrieved the beaver, and the Maryland Department of Health confirmed the rabies diagnosis the following day. Authorities have prohibited swimming in the William Houck Area until it can be deemed safe, a process that may take several days. Health officials are urging anyone who had contact with the beaver or other wildlife to seek medical attention and contact the Frederick County Health Department. Rabies, a viral disease spread through animal bites, is nearly always fatal in humans without immediate treatment.
Original source text (English)

Teenager swimming in Maryland lake bitten by rabid beaver Abigail Constantino | aconstantino@wtop.com July 27, 2026, 6:18 PM Share This: share on facebook share on X share on threads share on linkedin share on email print It happened Sunday around 9 a.m. as the teenager was in a designated swim area at Cunningham Falls State Park in Frederick County. A teenager who was swimming in a Maryland lake was bitten by a beaver that tested positive for the rabies virus. Related stories Health officials warn of potential rabies exposure in Loudoun County, elsewhere Rabies alert issued by Alexandria Health Department after fox tests positive Fox tests positive for rabies in Alexandria It happened Sunday around 9 a.m. as the teenager was in a designated swim area at Cunningham Falls State Park in Frederick County. Animal control picked up the beaver later that day from the Maryland Department of Natural Resources. On Monday, the Maryland Department of Health notified the Frederick County Health Department that the beaver tested positive for rabies. Authorities said swimming is prohibited in the William Houck Area in Hunting Creek Lake until it can be confirmed safe, which could take several days. “Mammals can be infected with the rabies virus, therefore do not touch or play with unknown animals. If you or your pets have contact with a wild animal contact the local authorities,” said Barry Glotfelty, director of the county’s Environmental Health Services. If you believe you had contact with this beaver or any other wildlife, call your doctor and the Frederick County Health Department’s Community Health Services Office at 301-600-3342. For pets or livestock, call your veterinarian and notify the county’s Environmental Health Office at 301-600-1717. Rabies is a viral disease that mainly infects mammals and is spread through the saliva of the infected animal, usually by a bite, the Frederick County Health Department said in a news release. “Symptoms of rabies in animals may include fear of water, excessive salivation, failure to eat or drink, limping, unusually friendly or aggressive behavior and/or other atypical behaviors such as nocturnal animals emerging during the daytime,” the health department said. The disease is nearly always fatal to humans if infected and no prompt post-exposure treatment is had. B routine priorityPublic-health news

2 Ohio counties confirm bats with rabies - Spectrum News

News Brief
Ohio health officials in Coshocton and Highland counties have confirmed rabies-positive bats, urging public caution. While rabies is serious, treatment is nearly 100% effective if administered quickly through post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP). The state has seen 15 confirmed rabies cases this year, all from bats, with over 21,000 annual exposures reported. Residents are advised to safely capture bats using professional services or protective measures and seek immediate medical attention if exposed. Public health authorities stress the importance of prompt action while reassuring the community that panic is unnecessary.
Original source text (English)
COLUMBUS, Ohio — The Coshocton Public Health District and the Highland County Health Department confirmed Thursday that bats have tested positive for rabies. In Coshocton, one bat was confirmed with rabies in Franklin Township. The Highland County Health Department didn't state where the other bat was found. "Rabies is a serious disease, but there is no need for panic," said Kristina Bell, Coshocton Public Health Commissioner. "We want to remind our community to follow these steps, and seek medical help promptly if you have been exposed. Treatment for rabies is highly effective, if sought quickly." Those who believe they may have been exposed to rabies should seek immediate medical attention. Individuals will go through a process called post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) which includes wound care, human rabies immune globulin and a series of four or five rabies vaccines, which must be given as soon as possible after exposure, according to the department. The process is nearly 100% effective. If Ohioans find a bat in their homes, they're encouraged to take the following steps: - Close all windows and doors, and leave the bat in the room - Call a professional to capture the bat - If there is no professional to capture the bat, Miami County Public Health recommends putting on leather gloves, slowly approaching the bat when it lands, and placing a see-through container over the bat. Then, slide a lid under the container to trap the bat. Secure the lid with tape and punch small holes in the container to allow the bat to breathe As of June 8, there have been 15 confirmed rabies cases in Ohio and all from bats, according to the Ohio Department of Health. In mid-May, that number stood at seven. In 2025, there were 19 confirmed cases of bats with rabies across the state, a sharp decline from 2024, which had 43. In Ohio, there are more than 20,000 bite exposures and 1,000 non-bite exposures are reported in Ohio each year, exposing more than 21,000 people, according to ODH. Non-bite exposures can happen when a bat is found in a room with a child, someone who is sleeping or someone with a sensory or mental impairment
View five news elements
Time
Published on 2026-07-31; Event confirmed on Thursday (specific date not reported in evidence).
Location and population
Coshocton County (Franklin Township) and Highland County, Ohio; general public and residents.
Event
Confirmed rabies cases in bats reported by Coshocton Public Health District and Highland County Health Department.
Scale, impact and risk
15 confirmed rabies cases in Ohio as of June 8, all from bats; 21,000 annual exposures (bite and non-bite); risk described as serious but not requiring panic.
Response, status and uncertainty
Public health guidance provided for bat encounters and post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP); treatment emphasized as highly effective if sought promptly; source limitation: full original article reviewed.

Rabid raccoon confirmed in Charleston; exposure reported near Wappoo Drive and Hinson Ave - WCIV

News Brief
Charleston County health officials confirmed a rabid raccoon near Wappoo Drive and Hinson Avenue on July 31, 2026, with one person exposed and referred for treatment. This marks the second rabid raccoon in Charleston in under two weeks, following a case on July 21. Health authorities emphasize the importance of pet vaccinations and caution about potential bat exposures, noting that not all bats carry rabies but bites can be easily overlooked due to their small size.

Applications Public File Assistance Contact News Team Careers Contests ACCESSIBILITY Rabid raccoon confirmed in Charleston; exposure reported near Wappoo Drive and Hinson Ave by Ian Kayanja Fri, July 31, 2026 at 4:24 PM 3VIEW ALL PHOTOS FILE - A file image of a raccoon. (Photo credit: N.C. Wildlife Resources Commission) 0Comment Share topics: Rabies Raccoon Charleston South Carolina Wappoo Drive Hinson Avenue Greenville Bat CHARLESTON, S.C. (WCIV) — More cases of rabies among animals were recorded in South Carolina, according to newly released information from the state's Department of Public Health. On Friday, the agency confirmed a raccoon near Wappoo Drive and Hinson Avenue in Charleston tested positive for rabies. One person was exposed and has been referred to their healthcare provider, state officials said. It marks the second rabid raccoon uncovered in less than two weeks. On Tuesday, July 21, a rabid raccoon was located in the area of Erica Road and Quarter Hoss Lane in Hollywood. Two people in that case were exposed. The second case came from a bat near North Main and West Court streets in Greenville. One person was also exposed there and referred to their healthcare provider for treatment. “Rabid bats have been known to transmit the rabies virus,” said Terri McCollister, the rabies program manager. “People don’t always realize they or a pet have been bitten since bat teeth are tiny and bites are easy to overlook.” READ MORE | "Rabid raccoon confirmed in Orangeburg County after dog exposure, DPH says." To safeguard against rabies exposure, the public is encouraged always to assume a person or a pet has potentially been bitten by a rabid bat when they wake up to find a bat in a room or tent; a bat is found where children, pets, or persons with impaired mental capacity (intoxicated or mentally disabled) have been left unattended; or they have been in direct contact with a bat. McCollister, however, stressed that not all bats are infected with rabies. “Although bats can carry rabies, not every bat is infected with the virus," McCollister said. "Bats are an important part of South Carolina's ecosystems and deserve a healthy degree of respect just like all wild animals." The health department stressed the need to keep pets up to date on rabies vaccinations as the easiest way to protect against the disease. In 2026, this bat is the fourth animal in Greenville County to test positive for rabies and the Charleston raccoon is the eighth animal to test positive for rabies in that county. 一名南泽西男子被一只患有狂犬病的浣熊咬伤。格洛斯特县卫生官员证实,这只浣熊的 狂犬病 检测呈阳性,并建议公众避开野生动物,如果被咬或抓伤,请立即就医。
规模、影响与风险
一项证实人类接触过 狂犬病。浣熊是唯一确诊病例。卫生官员警告说,浣熊、狐狸、臭鼬和流浪动物可能带来潜在风险。如果在就医 治疗 之前出现症状,狂犬病 被描述为致命。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员发布了公共警告并建议采取预防措施,包括携带宠物 疫苗接种 和避免接触野生动物。提供了动物控制的联系信息。该男子接受了医疗救治,官员们强调了暴露后 治疗 的重要性。

South Jersey man bitten by rabid raccoon; health officials advise people to avoid wild animals - PhillyVoice

News Brief
A South Jersey man was bitten by a rabid raccoon in Glassboro, Gloucester County, on Sunday. The raccoon tested positive for rabies on Wednesday, prompting health officials to warn residents to avoid wild animals and seek immediate medical attention if bitten or scratched. Officials advised pet owners to ensure their pets are vaccinated and to report strange animal behavior to animal control. Rabies, a fatal viral disease affecting the central nervous system, spreads through saliva and requires prompt post-exposure treatment to prevent death. The incident highlights ongoing risks in the region despite low human cases nationally.

7 secret getaways in NJ Why your budget feels tighter 8/15-16: Mount Gretna Outdoor Art Show The comment behind IBX's new campaign Menu PhillyVoice Search News Politics Opinion Crime Odd News Education Business Health Health News Illness Children's Health Addiction Fitness Wellness Parenting Culture Food & Drink TV Music Entertainment Arts & Culture Events Fitness Family-Friendly Festivals Performances Food & Drink Shopping Sports Eagles Sixers Phillies Flyers Fantasy Football Staff / Contributors Company Info About Us Advertise Contact Us Legal / Privacy Staff / Contributors RSS © 2026 WWB Holdings, LLC. All rights reserved More Health: Health News Parenting Wellness Healthy Eating Mental Health Addiction Newsletters July 29, 2026 South Jersey man bitten by rabid raccoon; health officials advise people to avoid wild animals People are advised to seek immediate medical attention if they are scratched or bitten by an animal suspected of having rabies. By Courtenay Harris Bond PhillyVoice Staff Prevention Rabies from Jeffry Surianto/Pexels After a South Jersey man was bitten by rabid raccoon, Gloucester County officials advised people to avoid wild animals and take other precautions. The photo above is a stock image. Read More A raccoon that bit a South Jersey man on Sunday has tested positive for rabies. "I was walking to leave when a raccoon came out of the shrubbery and charged me," Kevin Tucker, of Glassboro, Gloucester County, told 6ABC on Tuesday. "I kind of fell backward, and I think that's when it bit the front part of my right leg." MORE: Penn bioethicist criticizes FDA panel's endorsement of six peptides On Wednesday, Gloucester County health officials warned people to avoid wild animals and keep their distance from stray dogs and cats. They also advised pet owners to ensure that their pets have been vaccinated against rabies. "Be aware that high risk wildlife such as raccoons, foxes and skunks also feed in areas where outside cats are fed," health officials warned. Anyone that sees an animal that is behaving strangely or acting disoriented is advised not to handle it and immediately call Gloucester County animal control at (856) 881-2828. What is rabies? Rabies is a viral disease that attacks the central nervous system and brain. It can be fatal if a person becomes symptomatic before getting medical attention. Rabies spreads through contact with the saliva of infected animals. Fewer than five people each year test positive for rabies, but many more people come into contact with rabid animals, according to Yale Medicine. Warm-blooded mammals are all susceptible to rabies, including raccoons, skunks, foxes, bats and dogs. Most dogs in the United States are vaccinated against rabies. Bats are the most common cause of the disease in the United States. Because of its strict quarantine laws, Hawaii is the only state that is free of rabies. When to get a rabies shot Rabies symptoms mimic flu symptoms, including fatigue, weakness, fever and headache, which can make diagnosis tricky. Signs of rabies may also include prickling, itching and discomfort where a person has been bitten. More advanced symptoms include hallucination, anxiety, agitation, extreme thirst and saliva production, and aggression and thrashing, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention says. It can be weeks or months after a bite from a rabid animal before the first symptoms of rabies appear – and about two weeks between early signs and extreme symptoms. But rabies is incurable and fatal once a person develops clinical symptoms, the CDC says. This is why it is crucial for people to seek immediate medical attention after being scratched or bitten by an animal that may be rabid. Clinicians at an emergency departments can determine whether a potentially exposed person needs a rabies shot. According to CDC guidelines, a person who has not been vaccinated against rabies should first receive a shot of human rabies immune globulin. This shot gives a person antibodies to fight off the virus until the subsequent vaccination takes hold. The rabies vaccine should be given at the first medical visit. Additional doses should be given three days later, seven days later and 14 days later. People with immune disorders may need a fifth shot, the CDC says. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bat tests positive for rabies in Coshocton County - fox8.com

News Brief
A bat tested positive for rabies in Coshocton County, Ohio, with local health officials confirming the case on Thursday. The bat was found in Franklin Township, and while no human exposures were confirmed, two exposed animals had been previously vaccinated. This is part of a larger trend, with 16 rabies-positive bats reported across 14 counties in 2026. Health officials emphasize rabies is a serious but preventable disease, urging the public to avoid wild animals and ensure pets are vaccinated. No further details on the investigation or response actions were disclosed.
Original source text (English)
Fox 8 Cleveland WJW Bat tests positive for rabies in Coshocton County Kevin Accettulla 1 day ago COSHOCTON, Ohio (WCMH) — A bat tested positive for rabies in Coshocton County, local health officials said Thursday. The Coshocton Public Health District was notified of the positive test on Wednesday by the Ohio Department of Health. The positive test was from Franklin Township, which includes Franklin, Conesville and Wills Creek. No human exposures were confirmed, according to the district. Two animals that were directly exposed both had previously been vaccinated for rabies. Watch the latest headlines from NBC4 in the player above. There have been 16 bats in 14 counties that have tested positive for rabies in 2026. Rabies is a deadly virus that can affect any mammal, including pets and humans. It attacks the nervous system and can spread through saliva, often through bites. “Rabies is a serious disease, but there is no need for panic,” Public Health District Commissioner Kristina Bell said in a statement. The health district recommends the community avoid contact with wild animals or strays, don’t approach animals that appear sick, injured or dead, and ensure pets are up-to-date on their rabies vaccines. 2026 年 30 日;事件发生:周四下午(N6、N7)。
地点与对象

卫生官员证实,卡斯特县 Valley View Road 附近发现的一只蝙蝠 狂犬病 呈阳性(N7、N8)。
规模、影响与风险
没有报告病例或死亡;通过蝙蝠唾液对潜在的 狂犬病 传播 进行公开警告,即使是看似微不足道的接触(N8、N9、N10),也强调了风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
公共卫生机构建议避免接触野生动物/流浪动物;已分享CDC预防措施;提供了蝙蝠采集指南以及在疑似暴露情况下的医疗护理建议;来源为完整原始报告(N8、N11、N12)。

Bat found in Custer County tests positive for rabies - KKTV

News Brief
Health officials in Custer County, Colorado, confirmed a bat found near Valley View Road tested positive for rabies on Thursday afternoon. The Custer County Public Health Agency is urging residents to avoid contact with wild and stray animals, as even minor bat contact could transmit rabies through saliva. The CDC recommends keeping pets vaccinated, staying away from wildlife, and seeking immediate medical care after potential exposure. Residents who find dead or behaving strangely bats are advised to contact authorities without direct contact. No human or animal cases have been reported, but public health measures are active to prevent transmission.

(KKTV) -On Thursday afternoon, health officials said a bat found near Valley View Road in Custer County tested positive for rabies. Custer County Public Health Agency is asking the community to avoid contact with all wild and stray animals, including bats, during this time. According to health officials, what could seem to be insignificant contact with a bat could still be enough to transmit rabies. Rabies commonly spreads through contact with an infected animal’s saliva as a result of bites or exposure to cuts, but in some instances, people may not realize they were bitten and may not see the wound. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention shared several tips to help control and prevent rabies: Making sure your pets are up to date on their rabies vaccines Keeping wildlife wild - stay away from wildlife for both human and animal safety Calling animal control to remove stray animals from your neighborhood Washing bites or scratches immediately with soap and water after possible rabies exposure Seeking medical care urgently if you may have been exposed to rabies If you find a dead bat or one that seems to be behaving strangely, you are urged to contact Custer County Public Health Agency or Colorado Parks and Wildlife. If you believe the bat had contact with a human or pet, especially in an enclosed building, collect the bat without physical contact and bring it to Custer County Public Health Agency. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

卫生部:随着野生动物进入城市地区,狂犬病 病例上升 - 耶路撒冷邮报

Health Ministry: Rabies cases rise as wild animals move into urban areas - The Jerusalem Post
新闻简报以色列卫生部报告称,野生动物中的 狂犬病 病例在过去四年中增加了两倍,2022 年至 2025 年间增加了 252%。到 2026 年,已诊断出 69 例,预计到年底将达到 150 例左右,而 2022 年仅为 29 例。这一惊人的增长归因于豺狼和野猪等野生动物侵入城市地区例如特拉维夫和海法,是由人类活动、建筑和野生动物流离失所驱动的。狂犬病 是一种致命疾病,在没有 死亡率 的情况下,死亡率 的死亡率接近 100%,是以色列野生动物种群中的地方病,通过咬伤或唾液接触传播。尽管由于采取了预防措施,人类死亡的情况很少见,但卫生部强调公众保持警惕,敦促在可能接触后立即清洗伤口并就医。随着城市化使人类更接近受感染的野生动物,这种情况凸显了日益严重的公共卫生风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 28 日。事件期间从 2022 年到 2026 年,过去四年报告的病例数显着增加。
地点与对象
以色列,特别是特拉维夫和海法等城市地区,豺狼和野猪等野生动物正在入侵。受影响的人群包括暴露于潜在 狂犬病 传播 的野生动物和人类。
事件
以色列野生动物种群中的 狂犬病 病例已确认增加,在过去四年中增加了两倍多。卫生部对 狂犬病 的“惊人增长”和向城市地区的传播表示“非常关注”。
规模、影响与风险
2022 年至 2025 年间,野生动物中的 狂犬病 病例增加了 252%。2026 年,已诊断出 69 只动物,预计到年底总数约为 150 例,高于 2022 年的 29 例。这种疾病是致命的,在没有 死亡率 的情况下,死亡率 的发病率接近 100%,尽管由于采取预防措施,以色列的人类死亡很少见措施。风险包括人类通过动物咬伤或唾液接触而可能接触到的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生部正在积极监测情况,并发布了公共卫生指南,包括伤口清洗程序,并敦促与当地卫生办公室或急诊部门联系以了解潜在的暴露情况。该部还在调查导致传播的因素,例如人类喂养方式和野生动物流离失所。证据中没有详细说明具体的 治疗 或根除计划。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Health Ministry: Rabies cases rise as wild animals move into urban areas - The Jerusalem Post

News Brief
Israel's Health Ministry has reported a tripling of rabies cases in wild animals over the past four years, with a 252% increase observed between 2022 and 2025. By 2026, 69 cases have been diagnosed, with projections reaching around 150 by year-end, up from just 29 in 2022. The alarming rise is attributed to wild animals like jackals and wild boars encroaching on urban areas such as Tel Aviv and Haifa, driven by human activity, construction, and wildlife displacement. Rabies, a fatal disease with near 100% mortality without treatment, is endemic in Israel's wild animal population and spreads through bites or saliva contact. While human deaths are rare due to preventative measures, the ministry has emphasized public vigilance, urging immediate wound washing and medical consultation after potential exposures. The situation underscores growing public health risks as urbanization brings humans closer to infected wildlife.
Original source text (English)
CONFERENCES ISRAEL NEWS WORLD NEWS MIDDLE EAST BUSINESS & INNOVATION DEFENSE & TECH OPINION JP STORE Login LoginSubscribe JPOST DIGITAL LIBRARY HOME PAGE THE JERUSALEM REPORT DEFENSE & TECH JPOST HIGHER EDUCATION OPINION BUSINESS & INNOVATION JERUSALEM POST EN ESPANOL REAL ESTATE LISTINGS ALIYAH JERUSALEM POST CONFERENCE PREMIUM JP STORE Categories ISRAEL NEWS WORLD NEWS MIDDLE EAST US POLITICS DIASPORA OPINION PODCAST JUDAISM KABBALAH CHRISTIAN WORLD HEALTH & WELLNESS SCIENCE LAW ARCHAEOLOGY OMG ENVIRONMENT & CLIMATE CHANGE FOOD & RECIPES HISTORY SPONSORED CONTENT ADVERTISE WITH US TERMS OF USE PRIVACY POLICY CONTACT US CANCEL SUBSCRIPTION CUSTOMER SERVICE ABOUT US Jerusalem Post/Health & Wellness Rabies cases tripled over past years as wild animals move into urban areas, Health Ministry says The ministry is “very concerned” over rabies, which has shown an “alarming increase” in wild animals, some of whom are reaching urban areas, including jackals in Tel Aviv and wild boars in Haifa. Follow us on Google Wild boars gather in a residential area in the northern Israeli city of Haifa on December 5, 2019. (photo credit: Menahem Kahana/AFP via Getty Images) ByDANYA SAPERSTEIN JULY 28, 2026 12:08 Rabies cases have more than tripled in the past four years, spreading throughout the country, according to a Health Ministry statement released on Tuesday. The Health Ministry is “very concerned” over rabies, which has shown an “alarming increase” in wild animals, some of whom are encroaching on urban areas, including jackals in Tel Aviv and wild boars in Haifa. Rabies is a fatal disease endemic to Israel’s wild animal population. Once an animal or person is infected, without preventative treatment, the mortality rate is almost 100%. While preventative treatment has made human deaths from rabies in Israel rare, with only four after the state’s first decade, tens of thousands of people worldwide die from the disease each year. The disease attacks the central nervous system of mammals and is transmitted through bites or through contact between saliva and broken skin or mucous membranes. A bite, lick, or any other contact with the saliva of an infected animal can transmit the virus during a later stage of the disease, in most animals, within 10 days before their death. In recent years, rabies cases in Israel’s wild animal population have risen sharply, with a 252% increase between 2022 and 2025. In 2026, 69 animals have already been diagnosed, and the Health Ministry expects the number to reach around 150 by the end of the year. By comparison, this is up from 29 cases in 2022. A jackal is seen at Hayarkon Park in Tel Aviv, on September 03, 2022. (credit: TOMER NEUBERG/FLASH90) Human activity leads to geographic spread of infected animals The increase is partially due to the geographic spread of infected animals. Rabies can spread to a region through both animal movement and feeding practices. Intentionally leaving out food for stray cats may also feed other wild animals, as can leaving garbage in the street and poor sanitation. The Health Ministry warned that urban density, construction, and the displacement of wildlife from their natural habitats all contribute to the problem. Additionally, infected animals may be transported by people. In 2026, there were three such cases: a reservist who brought a dog from northern Israel to Hadera and Ra’anana, where it came into contact with his extended family over Passover, a dog who was brought from Ramallah to Holon, and an adopted dog that had never been vaccinated or examined by a veterinarian who traveled by train from Jerusalem to Tel Aviv, where it was brought to the beach. That incident ultimately required the Health Ministry to manage 52 exposure cases. The Health Ministry emphasized that in the event of an animal injury, one should first wash the affected area thoroughly with soap and water for about 10 minutes. They should then urgently contact their local health office to assess the level of risk and determine whether vaccination or other preventative treatment is necessary. No appointment is required for such a visit. When local health offices are closed, people should go to a hospital emergency department for initial treatment. View five news elements
Time
Published on July 28, 2026. Event period spans from 2022 to 2026, with a notable increase in cases reported over the past four years.
Location and population
Israel, specifically urban areas including Tel Aviv and Haifa, where wild animals such as jackals and wild boars are encroaching. The affected population includes both wild animals and humans exposed to potential rabies transmission.
Event
Confirmed increase in rabies cases in Israel's wild animal population, with cases more than tripling over the past four years. The Health Ministry has expressed 'very concerned' status over the 'alarming increase' and the spread of rabies to urban areas.
Scale, impact and risk
Rabies cases in wild animals rose by 252% between 2022 and 2025. In 2026, 69 animals have been diagnosed, with an expected total of around 150 by year-end, up from 29 cases in 2022. The disease is fatal with near 100% mortality rate without treatment, though human deaths in Israel are rare due to preventative measures. Risk includes potential human exposure through animal bites or saliva contact.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Health Ministry is actively monitoring the situation and has issued public health guidelines, including wound washing procedures and urging contact with local health offices or emergency departments for potential exposures. The ministry is also investigating factors contributing to the spread, such as human feeding practices and wildlife displacement. No specific treatment or eradication plans are detailed in the evidence.
