美国农业部开展 2026 年口头 狂犬病 疫苗接种 针对美国东部野生动物的努力 - Morning Ag Clips

USDA Conducts 2026 Oral Rabies Vaccination Efforts Targeting Wildlife in Eastern United States - Morning Ag Clips
新闻简报The USDA is conducting its 2026 oral rabies vaccination program in the eastern United States to prevent the spread of raccoon rabies. Starting July 31, APHIS will distribute millions of RABORAL V-RG® and ONRAB baits targeting wildlife like raccoons, gray foxes, and striped skunks. The program spans multiple states, with baits distributed by air and ground through October. Rabies is nearly always fatal but preventable, with 90% of U.S. cases in wildlife. The vaccines are safe for pets and humans, though public guidance advises leaving undisturbed baits intact. This annual effort aims to protect public health and reduce the $500 million annual cost of rabies control.
查看新闻五要素
时间
于 2026 年 7 月 31 日发布。疫苗接种 工作自发布之日起开始,并将持续到 8 月(东北部到大西洋中部各州)、9 月中旬至 10 月中旬(马萨诸塞州)和 10 月(南部各州)。
地点与对象
美国东部,包括纽约州、佛蒙特州、缅因州、俄亥俄州、西弗吉尼亚州、马萨诸塞州、北卡罗来纳州、田纳西州、弗吉尼亚州、佐治亚州和阿拉巴马州的特定地区。目标人群是野生动物,特别是浣熊、灰狐和条纹臭鼬。
事件
农业部动植物卫生检验局 (APHIS) 正在开展年度口服 狂犬病 狂犬病 (ORV) 计划,通过分发 RABORAL V-RG® 和 ONRAB 诱饵来防止浣熊 狂犬病 扩散到美国中心地带。这是一项已确认的、有计划的公共卫生干预措施。
规模、影响与风险
Millions of ORV baits are distributed annually. The 2026 distribution includes specific quantities for each project area (e.g., 220,000 baits in Watertown, New York; 598,000 in Houlton, Maine). Rabies is a serious public health concern, with about 90% of reported rabies cases in the United States occurring in wildlife. Annual costs for rabies detection, prevention, and control may exceed $500 million.
应对、状态与不确定性
APHIS 和合作者正在通过飞机、直升机、车辆和诱饵站分发诱饵。RABORAL V-RG® 疫苗 对于家养狗和猫来说是安全的,人类和宠物不会因接触诱饵而感染 狂犬病。ONRAB 的实地评估正在选定的地区进行。为遇到诱饵提供公共指导。

USDA Conducts 2026 Oral Rabies Vaccination Efforts Targeting Wildlife in Eastern United States While rabies is almost always fatal once symptoms appear, it also is 100% preventable PUBLISHED ON July 31, 2026 USDA-APHIS Wildlife Services’ National Rabies Management Program distributes millions of oral rabies vaccine baits each year to help prevent the spread of this deadly disease in wildlife, such as raccoons, gray foxes, and striped skunks. (USDA photo by Wildlife Service, Public domain) WASHINGTON — The U.S. Department of Agriculture’s Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service (APHIS) will begin its annual distribution of RABORAL V-RG®, an oral rabies vaccine (ORV) bait, in select areas in the eastern United States to prevent the spread of raccoon rabies into America’s heartland. Rabies is a serious public health concern. While rabies is almost always fatal once symptoms appear, it also is 100% preventable. Human exposures can be successfully remedied if medical attention is sought immediately following exposure. Costs associated with rabies detection, prevention, and control may exceed $500 million annually in the United States. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), about 90 percent of reported rabies cases in the United States are in wildlife. APHIS and its cooperators will begin distributing ORV baits today across rural areas by airplane and in suburban or urban areas by helicopter, vehicle, and bait station. RABORAL V-RG® rabies baits are coated with a fishmeal attractant and are packaged in two-inch plastic sachets. The RABORAL V-RG® vaccine is safe for many animals, including domestic dogs and cats. Humans and pets cannot get rabies from contact with the baits but should leave them undisturbed if they are encountered. Dogs that consume many baits may experience a temporary upset stomach, but there are no long-term health risks. If people come in contact with baits, they should immediately wash the contact area with warm water and soap. ORV baits will be distributed during the following time periods and based out of airports in the cities listed below: In Northeast to Mid-Atlantic states during August: The Watertown, New York, project will include portions of northern New York in the St. Lawrence Valley region and distribute 220,000 baits by airplane, helicopter and vehicles. In addition to RABORAL V-RG®, APHIS will distribute another ORV bait, ONRAB, in select areas of New York as part of an ongoing field assessment. The Barre, Vermont, project will encompass parts of northern New Hampshire, northeastern New York and northern Vermont and distribute almost 460,000 baits by airplanes and vehicles. APHIS will also be distributing ONRAB in this region. The Houlton, Maine, project will cover parts of northern Maine and distribute approximately 598,000 ORV baits by airplane and vehicle. The Jamestown, New York, project will include areas in western New York and distribute approximately 379,000 ORV baits by airplane and helicopter. Field assessment of ONRAB will occur in this area concurrently. The North Lima, Ohio, project will involve distribution of approximately 729,000 RABORAL V-RG® baits in parts of western Pennsylvania. Field assessment of ONRAB will also occur in western Pennsylvania at the same time. The Buckhannon, West Virginia, project will include portions of western Pennsylvania and West Virginia, distributing approximately 364,000 ORV baits by airplane and vehicle. ONRAB field assessment also will take place in select areas of West Virginia. In Massachusetts from mid-September through mid-October: The Cape Cod, Mass., project will cover parts of peninsular and mainland Massachusetts and distribute 75,000 ORV baits by helicopter, bait station, and vehicle. In Southern states during October: The Abingdon, Va., project will cover parts of North Carolina, Tennessee, Virginia and a small portion of southern West Virginia, distributing more than 686,000 ORV baits by airplane, helicopter, and vehicle. The Andrews, N.C., project will include areas in western North Carolina and north Georgia and will distribute approximately 415,000 ORV baits by airplane, helicopter and vehicle. The Dalton, Ga., project will cover areas in Alabama, Georgia, North Carolina, and Tennessee, distributing 648,000 ORV baits by airplane and helicopter. The Gadsden, Ala., project will cover parts of northeast and central Alabama and distribute approximately 712,000 baits by airplane, helicopter, and vehicle. For additional information concerning rabies or the ORV program, please visit www.aphis.usda.gov/national-wildlife-programs/rabies or contact APHIS Wildlife Services toll free at 1-866-4-USDA-WS (1-866-487-3297). —USDA APHIS Spread the word Shares RECOMMENDED ARTICLES Removing Stigma & Building Mental Wellness in Farm Communities May 12, 2026 MAYVILLE, N.Y. — The next Rural Mental Health Connections Webinar, “Removing Stigma & Building Mental Wellness in Farm Communities,” will take place online May 28, 2026. It is free and open to anyone interested in learning more about the challenges facing farmers and farm communities, along with ways rural residents can support each other and promote mental wellness. Rabid bat confirmed in Coshocton County; no human exposure reported - Coshocton Tribune

Rabid bat confirmed in Coshocton County; no human exposure reported - Coshocton Tribune

News Brief
Health officials in Coshocton County confirmed a rabid bat in Franklin Township on July 29, 2026, with no human exposure reported. The Coshocton Public Health District, notified by the Ohio Department of Health, emphasized that exposed animals were vaccinated and monitored. This case is part of a broader trend, with 15 rabid bats detected across 14 Ohio counties in 2026. Rabies, transmitted through saliva via bites or contact with open wounds, poses a serious risk but is preventable. Authorities urge residents to avoid wild animals, report unusual behavior, and ensure pets are vaccinated, stressing that prompt treatment is highly effective.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Life & Events Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals COSHOCTON COUNTY Rabid bat confirmed in Coshocton County; no human exposure reported Leonard L. Hayhurst Coshocton Tribune July 31, 2026, 9:00 a.m. ET COSHOCTON − A bat in Franklin Township of Coshocton County has tested positive for rabies, but there was no confirmed exposure to humans. The Coshocton Public Health District said it was notified on July 29 of the positive test by the Ohio Department of Health, according to a community announcement. The announcement said animals that alerted the resident to the bat had direct exposure and were being monitored by local health officials. Both animals had received rabies vaccinations. According to the ODH, 15 additional bats in 14 counties have tested positive for rabies in 2026, including adjacent Tuscarawas and Muskingum counties. The disease attacks the nervous system, including the brain and spinal cord. Bats are the most common source of rabies exposure in Ohio, because bites are small and difficult to detect. It's also commonly found in raccoons and skunks. Rabies is transmitted via saliva through contact by a bite, open wound or mucus membrane in the eyes, nose and mouth. The health district recommends avoiding contact with wild or stray animals and never approach any sick, injured or dead animals. Residents should report any unusual animal behavior to local authorities and ensure pets are current on rabies vaccines. "Rabies is a serious disease, but there is no need for panic," Coshocton Public Health District Commissioner Kristina Bell said in the announcement. "We want to remind our community to follow these steps and seek medical help promptly if you have been exposed. Beaver tests positive for rabies after biting 13-year-old at Cunningham Falls State Park - CBS News

News Brief
A beaver at Cunningham Falls State Park in Maryland tested positive for rabies after biting a 13-year-old boy on Sunday, July 26, 2026. The incident occurred at the park's William Houck area near the North Beach of the lake at 9:10 a.m. The boy sustained injuries to his hands and chest and was flown to Johns Hopkins Hospital for treatment. Following the incident, the swimming beach was immediately closed, and Maryland authorities located and tested the beaver, which confirmed rabies. As of Monday, July 27, the swimming area remains closed until deemed safe. Park staff are investigating the presence of additional beavers, and the Frederick County Health Department is advising anyone who had contact with the beaver or other wild animals to consult their healthcare provider.

Rabid bat found in New Jersey attic leaves 3 cats exposed to rabies - The Cool Down

News Brief
A rabid bat discovered in the attic of a Haddonfield, New Jersey home on April 18, 2026, led to three cats being exposed to rabies, though no humans were affected. Testing confirmed the bat had rabies, prompting health officials to monitor the cats for 45 days. The cats, already vaccinated, received booster shots. Rabies remains a serious but preventable illness, with New Jersey reporting 174 cases last year and 26 more by March 2026. The last known human rabies case in the state was in 1997. Homeowners are advised to seal potential entry points and contact professionals for wildlife encounters.

Photo Credit: iStock A rabid bat found in the attic of a New Jersey home is alarming residents, showing that even ordinary household spaces can become unexpected points of contact among people, pets, and wildlife. Officials thankfully said no people were exposed, but the household's three cats now face a weeks-long monitoring period after encountering the infected bat inside the home. What happened? According to reporting from The Philadelphia Inquirer, testing later showed that a bat removed from the attic of the Haddonfield home on April 18 had rabies. After it was taken from the house without incident, the animal was sent to Trenton for testing. "There were no human exposures. Three cats were exposed to the bat," the county health department said in a press notice. The department also explained that "Rabies is a serious illness, but it can be prevented by early treatment after exposure. If you have been bitten or scratched by a wild animal, it is important that you seek immediate medical attention." B routine priorityPublic-health news

Health officials warn public after rabies cases in SC - WYFF

News Brief
Health officials in South Carolina have confirmed two rabies cases—a raccoon in Charleston County and a bat in Greenville County—resulting in two separate human exposures. Both individuals have been referred to healthcare providers for treatment. This year, South Carolina has reported 66 rabid animal cases statewide. Officials urge the public to contact local health departments if they suspect exposure to rabies, emphasizing that laboratory testing is necessary to confirm the disease, as visual inspection is unreliable. The bat was found near North Main and West Court Streets in Greenville, while the raccoon was located near Wappoo Drive and Hinson Avenue in Charleston.
Original source text (English)
Two exposed after rabid raccoon, bat confirmed in South Carolina Watch: 7.31 WYFF News 4 Morning Headlines 7.31 WYFF News 4 Morning Headlines WYFF US LIVE Current Time 0:00 / Total Duration 5:57 Sorry, this video is not available, please check back later. Advertisement Updated: 4:33 PM EDT Jul 31, 2026 Editorial Standards i Chloe McCoy Digital Content Producer Two exposed after rabid raccoon, bat confirmed in South Carolina Updated: 4:33 PM EDT Jul 31, 2026 Editorial Standards i Chloe McCoy Digital Content Producer GREENVILLE, S.C. — Two rabid animals have been confirmed in South Carolina, and health officials say two people have been exposed. According to the South Carolina Department of Health, a raccoon in Charleston County and a bat in Greenville County tested positive for rabies. Advertisement In Greenville, the bat was found near North Main and West Court Streets while the raccoon was found near Wappoo Drive and Hinson Avenue in Charleston. One person in each separate instance was exposed and referred to their healthcare provider for treatment. Officials ask that if you believe that you, someone you know, or your pets have been exposed to rabies contact SCDPH's Upstate Greenville-Spartanburg office at 864-372-3270 or the Charleston office at 843-953-4713 during normal business hours. SCDPH says as a reminder that you cannot tell if a bat, or any other animal, has rabies by simply looking at it as the disease must be confirmed in a laboratory. Unusual behavior in bats that might indicate the animal has rabies includes daytime activity, inability to fly, and being found in places they are not usually seen, like in your home or on your lawn. In 2026, this bat is the fourth animal in Greenville County to test positive for rabies and the Charleston raccoon is the seventh animal to test positive for rabies in that county. There have been 66 cases of rabid animals statewide this year, according to officials. 缅因州的蝙蝠和 狂犬病:如果您接触过病毒该怎么办

Bats and rabies in Maine: What to do if you’ve been exposed
新闻简报缅因州的蝙蝠季节正在进行中,截至 7 月中旬,缅因州疾病预防控制中心确认五个县有 5 只 狂犬病 阳性蝙蝠。虽然人类 狂犬病 病例在该州极为罕见(最后一次报告于 1937 年),但该机构强调由于蝙蝠活动增加而采取预防措施。蝙蝠可以通过咬伤或抓伤传播 狂犬病,接触风险包括在有人睡觉或幼儿的房间里发现蝙蝠。如果怀疑接触蝙蝠,建议公众安全捕获蝙蝠进行检测,彻底清洗伤口并寻求医疗评估。暴露后预防 每年向约 100,000 名美国人提供和管理。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 29 日。活动期间:仲夏至 9 月初(缅因州蝙蝠最活跃的季节)。
地点与对象
受影响人群:公众、宠物、牲畜。有确诊病例的具体县:安德罗斯科金、坎伯兰、肯纳贝克、佩诺布斯科特、萨默塞特。
事件
蝙蝠中确诊 狂犬病 病例。缅因州疾病预防控制中心宣布,截至 2026 年 7 月 17 日,有 5 只 狂犬病 阳性蝙蝠,五个县各有一只。这是一次公共卫生指导活动。
规模、影响与风险
5 bats tested positive for rabies in 2026 (out of 353 tested animals, just over half bats). In 2025, 7 bats tested positive (out of 730 tested animals). Human rabies is extremely rare in Maine (last case in 1937), but about 1.4 million Americans seek healthcare for potential rabies exposure annually. Risk level: Not explicitly stated, but precautions emphasized due to bat activity.
应对、状态与不确定性
缅因州 CDC 提供有关预防措施、检测程序(联系缅因州监狱长服务或动物控制官员)和 暴露后预防 的公共指导。不确定性:并非所有受感染的动物都表现出 狂犬病 的迹象,并且暴露可能并不总是明显的(例如,蝙蝠在有丧失行为能力的个体的房间里)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bats and rabies in Maine: What to do if you’ve been exposed

News Brief
Maine's bat season is underway, with the Maine CDC confirming 5 rabies-positive bats across five counties as of mid-July. While human rabies cases are exceedingly rare in the state (last reported in 1937), the agency emphasizes precautions due to increased bat activity. Bats can transmit rabies through bites or scratches, and exposure risks include finding bats in rooms with sleeping individuals or young children. The public is advised to safely capture bats for testing if exposure is suspected, wash wounds thoroughly, and seek medical evaluation. Post-exposure prophylaxis is available and administered to about 100,000 Americans annually.
Original source text (English)

Posted July 29 Updated July 30 Drew JohnsonStaff Writer 3 min readFont size + Gift Article Loading... You are able to gift 5 more articles this month. Anyone can access the link you share with no account required. Learn more. Email Copy Link Email address Send Article link sent! An error has occurred. Please try again. Subscribe Today Sign In Share this: Print (Opens in new window) Print Share Share on Facebook (Opens in new window) Facebook Share on Nextdoor (Opens in new window) Nextdoor Share on Bluesky (Opens in new window) Bluesky Share on LinkedIn (Opens in new window) LinkedIn Share on X (Opens in new window) X Email a link to a friend (Opens in new window) Email The Maine Center for Disease Control and Prevention announced Wednesday that a handful of bats have tested positive for rabies so far this year. With midsummer marking the start of bats’ most active season in Maine (ending in early September), the agency shared precautions people should take should they be exposed to the flying mammals. How many bats have tested positive for rabies in Maine? As of July 17, the Maine CDC has tested 353 animals this year, just over half of them bats. Five bats have tested positive for rabies, the agency reported Wednesday, one in each of the following counties: Androscoggin, Cumberland, Kennebec, Penobscot and Somerset. In 2025, the Maine CDC tested 730 animals, more than 340 of them bats. Seven bats tested positive for rabies that year. The Maine Department of Health and Human Services has a rabies dashboard on its website with data on bats and other animals that have been tested. Advertisement About 4,000 animals in the United States test positive for rabies each year, according to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, roughly 35% of them bats. How common is rabies in humans? Human cases of rabies are extremely rare in Maine and the nation as a whole. One to three cases of human rabies are reported in the United States each year, according to the U.S. CDC. However, according to the federal agency, about 1.4 million people seek healthcare for potential rabies exposure in the United States each year. Maine has not reported a human case of rabies since 1937, according to the Maine CDC. Rabies can be prevented after exposure with post-exposure prophylaxis. About 100,000 Americans receive post-exposure prophylaxis each year, according to the CDC. How do animals spread rabies? Animals spread rabies through bites and sometimes scratches, according to the Maine CDC. It can also spread through contact with an infected mammal’s brain or spinal cord. Advertisement Rabies does not spread through blood, urine, feces, dried saliva or skunk spray, the Maine CDC said. When should I take precautions? The Maine CDC notes that it’s not always easy for someone to confirm whether they have come into contact with a bat, and not all infected animals show signs of rabies. Beyond bites and scratches, the agency says humans should be wary of exposure when they or others are incapacitated. That includes waking up and finding a bat in their room; or locating bats in the vicinity of those who may not be able to say whether they’ve come into contact, such as young children. It’s also recommended to keep a close eye on pets and livestock, and take precautions if they’ve been in a confined space with bats (like a room or barn) or you find a bat in their mouth. What should I do if I’ve been exposed to a bat? If you think a person or animal may have been exposed to a bat, the Maine CDC provides several steps to take. The first, if possible, is to safely capture the bat. If you can manage that, the next step is to contact the Maine Warden Service to get it tested for rabies. If a warden is not available, the Maine CDC suggests contacting your local Animal Control Officer to arrange a test. If you have been bitten or scratched by a bat, the Maine CDC advises washing the area with soap and warm water for 10 to 15 minutes and contacting a healthcare provider. For pets and livestock that have come into contact with a bat, the agency says to consult a veterinarian and always keep up to date on their rabies vaccinations. Event
Confirmed rabies cases in bats. The Maine CDC announced 5 rabies-positive bats as of July 17, 2026, one in each of five counties. This is a public health guidance event.
Scale, impact and risk
5 bats tested positive for rabies in 2026 (out of 353 tested animals, just over half bats). In 2025, 7 bats tested positive (out of 730 tested animals). Human rabies is extremely rare in Maine (last case in 1937), but about 1.4 million Americans seek healthcare for potential rabies exposure annually. Risk level: Not explicitly stated, but precautions emphasized due to bat activity.
Response, status and uncertainty

Rabid groundhog underneath parked car attacks child in Pennsylvania - The Cool Down
新闻简报在宾夕法尼亚州南蒂科克的一条住宅街道上,一只患有狂犬病的土拨鼠袭击了一名儿童,当地警方就野生动物风险发出警告。这只动物在一辆停放的汽车下被发现,健康状况不佳,在被当局保护并运送进行检测后,被证实患有狂犬病。虽然孩子的伤势尚未得到官方证实,但警方注意到可能有咬伤和抓伤。这一事件凸显了接近生病或具有攻击性的野生动物的危险,因为一旦出现症状,狂犬病 几乎是致命的。宾夕法尼亚州通常每年报告数百起患有狂犬病的动物,但人类病例极为罕见,最近一次报告是在 1984 年。公共卫生官员敦促立即就可能的接触进行医疗咨询,并建议联系当局,而不是直接干预野生动物。

规模、影响与风险

警方赶到现场,保护了这只动物,并将其运送进行测试。南蒂科克市警察局发布了关于接近野生动物的公开警告。孩子的暴露状况尚未得到证实,但州指导将暴露定义为被患有狂犬病的动物咬伤或抓伤。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Rabid groundhog underneath parked car attacks child in Pennsylvania - The Cool Down

News Brief
On a residential street in Nanticoke, Pennsylvania, a rabid groundhog attacked a child, prompting a local police warning about wildlife risks. The animal, found in poor health under a parked car, was confirmed rabid after being secured and transported for testing by authorities. While the child's injuries were not officially confirmed, police noted possible bites and scratches. This incident highlights the dangers of approaching sick or aggressive wildlife, as rabies is nearly fatal once symptoms appear. Pennsylvania typically reports hundreds of rabid animals yearly, though human cases are exceedingly rare, with the last reported in 1984. Public health officials urge immediate medical consultation for potential exposures and advise contacting authorities rather than intervening directly with wildlife.
Original source text (English)

Tech Rabid groundhog underneath parked car attacks child in Pennsylvania The animal was found in poor health and later confirmed to be rabid. by Aaron GoldsteinAugust 1, 2026 share Facebook Twitter Link Copied! Photo Credit: iStock A rabid groundhog in Eastern Pennsylvania attacked a child on a residential street, prompting a warning from local police about the dangers of approaching wildlife that appears sick or aggressive. As human neighborhoods and wild animals' habitats increasingly overlap, even familiar backyard species can pose serious health risks. What happened? After the child was attacked in Nanticoke, police responded to the scene. The animal was found in poor health and later confirmed to be rabid, as WVIA reported. Thankfully, officers safely secured the animal and had it transported to the Pennsylvania Game Commission for testing. "A groundhog which was under a parked vehicle attacked a juvenile," the Nanticoke City Police Department wrote in a statement on Facebook. "... This groundhog was confirmed to be rabid. This post is to inform the public not to approach wild animals, and to protect your pets from them." Under state health guidance, a person is considered exposed to rabies if a rabid animal bites or scratches them. Chief of Police Michael Roke told WVIA that it appeared as if the child was bitten on the leg and scratched, though he added that he could not confirm this. TCD Picks » Good Store Spotlight Get 10% off your next purchase with code TCD10 Get sparkly dishes the right way, every time A great alternative to plastic coated dishwasher pods Tackle laundry stains with simple ingredients Ultra concentrated and plastic free for easy cleaning Up your scrub with all-natural ingredients This soap is packed with healing oils and butters Transform your everyday cleaning rituals 100% of all profit goes to charitable causes Why does it matter? Among diseases people can catch from wildlife, rabies is especially dangerous because survival is exceedingly rare once symptoms appear. State guidance cited by WVIA notes early symptoms can include fever, headache, fatigue, irritability, and pain or itching at the exposure site before the illness progresses to paralysis, seizures, delirium, and death. Warning signs for rabies in animals can include aggression, excessive drooling, vocalizations, lack of coordination, paralysis, and lack of fear around people. Pennsylvania typically confirms 350 to 500 rabid animals each year, according to WVIA. But human cases are far rarer. The most recent one reported dates all the way back to 1984. If you are bitten or scratched by a rabid animal or think you may have been exposed to one, contact a doctor immediately. The disease can be treated if caught quickly. Keep your distance from all wildlife, especially animals that behave oddly, whether that means they are disoriented, aggressive, or unafraid of humans. Those behaviors can signal illness, including rabies. Instead of intervening yourself, contact local authorities or wildlife officials, and make sure to keep pets and children out of harm's way. Rabid bat confirmed in downtown Greenville - FOX Carolina News

News Brief
The South Carolina Department of Public Health (DPH) has confirmed a rabid bat in downtown Greenville, near North Main Street and West Court Street. One person was exposed to the bat, which was tested and confirmed positive for rabies. Officials are urging anyone who believes they or someone they know may have come into contact with the bat to call DPH's Upstate Greenville-Spartanburg office. The DPH emphasizes that rabid bats can transmit rabies, and bites may be difficult to detect due to the small size of bat teeth. Public health measures are being taken to assess potential exposure and prevent further transmission.

(FOX Carolina) - The South Carolina Department of Public Health (DPH) confirmed one person was exposed to a rabid bat in downtown Greenville. Officials said the bat was found near North Main Street and West Court Street. It was submitted to DPH’s lab for testing on Wednesday and was confirmed to have rabies on Thursday. If you believe you or someone you know has come into contact with the bat, please call DPH’s Upstate Greenville-Spartanburg office at 864-372-3270. “Rabid bats have been known to transmit the rabies virus,” said Terri McCollister, Rabies Program manager. “People don’t always realize they or a pet have been bitten since bat teeth are tiny and bites are easy to overlook.” The DPH said you should always assume a person or pet has potentially been bitten when: They wake up to find a bat in a room or tent A bat is found where children, pets, or persons with impaired mental capacity (intoxicated or mentally disabled) have been left unattended They have been in direct contact with a bat Feel more informed, prepared, and connected with FOX Carolina. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Oregon matches record-high number of bats infected with rabies - Your Oregon News

News Brief
Oregon health officials are urging caution as bat rabies cases in the state have reached a record high in 2026, with 22 bats testing positive for the virus. This matches and may surpass the previous record set in 2006. Bats, which have carried rabies for thousands of years, are the primary source of human rabies exposures in the U.S. despite generally low infection rates. Officials emphasize that rabies is fatal without immediate post-exposure treatment and provide guidance on safely encountering bats, including avoiding contact and reporting exposures. The public is reminded to keep pets vaccinated and secure homes against bat entry.
Original source text (English)
Oregon matches record-high number of bats infected with rabies Published 9:10 pm Thursday, July 30, 2026 Oregon health officials are urging people to prevent exposure to rabies as positive tests continue to creep toward a new record high. The Oregon Health Authority and the Oregon Department of Fish and Wildlife have had 22 bats test positive for rabies in 2026. That means the state has matched, and is poised to surpass, its record number of bats with rabies in a single year. Bats have carried the rabies virus within their populations for thousands of years and are known to be common carriers today — though generally at very low levels. Despite that, they account for the most human exposures to the virus of any other species in the United States. The highest number of animals testing positive for rabies since 2000 was in 2006. That year, 22 bats and 2 foxes were found to be carrying the disease. In 2023, 20 bats tested positive. “When people find a dead or dying bat, they may pick it up with their hands because they want to help it, or maybe they’re just curious,” said Emilio DeBess, public health veterinarian at OHA’s Public Health Division. “When a bat or other wild animal is sick or dying with rabies, there’s an increased chance they will bite or transmit the virus in other ways.” Bats and other wildlife rarely bite people, but may do so if they are sick or feel threatened. “Bats provide important ecosystem services in Oregon, but also globally with insect control, seed dispersal, and pollination of specific plants, supporting agriculture and forest systems,” said Colin Gillin, ODFW state wildlife veterinarian. “And like many animals that can carry disease, bats can also prevent or suppress disease by reducing mosquitoes and other vectors that carry human and animal pathogens.” What to do if you find a bat Bats are protected wildlife, which makes it illegal to harm or keep them. If you see a bat that appears to be sick: • Stay calm, do not touch it, and keep people and pets away. • When the sick or dead bat is indoors, do not release it. If safe to do so, place a container or box over the bat. • When the sick or dead bat is outdoors, cover it with a box or bucket to keep it in place if safe to do so. Finding a dead bat does not require action unless someone was bitten or scratched. • Contact your local health authority or a veterinarian if you believe you were exposed. • If multiple sick or dead bats are in an area, report it to the ODFW: https://tinyurl.com/4h6v8kdr • When a person or pet has been bitten or scratched by an animal that may have rabies, report the exposure immediately and seek medical care. • Make sure your dogs and cats’ vaccinations are up to date. • Protect your home from bats by covering vents, chimneys and other entry points with screens. Rabies Rabies is a viral disease that is deadly if people don’t receive medical care before symptoms start. It spreads through bites or scratches from an infected animal. The most commonly infected species are bats, skunks, raccoons and foxes. If you believe you were exposed to rabies, do not wait. Urgently seek medical attention. You will need a series of four to five rabies vaccinations, which must start as soon as possible. The disease is rare in people in the U.S., with fewer than 10 deaths reported each year. Initial symptoms include a flu-like illness and discomfort at the bite site. Severe symptoms appear within two weeks. They include anxiety, confusion, agitation, hallucinations, thirst, but panic about fluids, lots of saliva, and aggressive behavior. Learn more from the Centers for Disease Control: cdc.gov/rabies/about/index.html

Residents Urged Not To Touch Bats As 2 Bats Test Positive For Rabies DuPage County - Patch
新闻简报杜佩奇县卫生官员在今年两只蝙蝠检测出 狂犬病 呈阳性后,敦促居民不要接触蝙蝠。该县报告有 82 人因可能接触 狂犬病 而接受治疗。蝙蝠是伊利诺伊州 狂犬病 的主要来源,大多数物种检测结果呈阴性,但任何蝙蝠都应被视为患有狂犬病,除非另有证明。卫生官员建议保护住所以防止蝙蝠进入,避免接触蝙蝠,并联系动物控制部门寻求帮助。建议居民如果被蝙蝠咬伤,请立即就医;如果可能接触过蝙蝠,请将宠物带去看兽医。还建议为宠物接种疫苗。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 31 日;该事件发生于 2026 年,今年迄今已发现两只 狂犬病 阳性蝙蝠。
地点与对象
美国伊利诺伊州杜佩奇县;可能接触 狂犬病 的居民和宠物。
事件
蝙蝠确诊 狂犬病 病例;杜佩奇县卫生局 (DCHD) 报告称,两只蝙蝠的 狂犬病 检测呈阳性,82 人因可能接触病毒而接受治疗。
规模、影响与风险
两只蝙蝠的 狂犬病 检测呈阳性;82 人因可能接触而接受治疗;蝙蝠是伊利诺伊州 狂犬病 的主要来源;来自蝙蝠的 狂犬病 传播 风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
DCHD 敦促居民不要接触蝙蝠,并联系动物控制中心寻求帮助;建议保护房屋以防止蝙蝠进入并为宠物接种疫苗;建议如果被蝙蝠咬伤或宠物可能接触过病毒,请立即就医。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Residents Urged Not To Touch Bats As 2 Bats Test Positive For Rabies DuPage County - Patch

News Brief
DuPage County health officials are urging residents not to touch bats after two bats tested positive for rabies this year. The county has reported 82 people treated for possible rabies exposure. Bats are the primary source of rabies in Illinois, with most species testing negative, but any bat should be considered rabid until proven otherwise. Health officials recommend securing homes to prevent bat entry, avoiding handling bats, and contacting animal control for assistance. Residents are advised to seek immediate medical attention if bitten by a bat and to take pets to a veterinarian if they may have been exposed. Vaccinating pets is also recommended.
Original source text (English)
Home & Garden Residents Urged Not To Touch Bats As 2 Bats Test Positive For Rabies DuPage County Two bats have tested positive for rabies in DuPage County so far this year, according to county health officials. Lisa Marie Farver , Patch Staff Posted Fri, Jul 31, 2026 at 12:17 am CT DUPAGE COUNTY, IL — "Never touch a bat," DuPage County Health Department (DCHD) cautions after two bats have tested positive for rabies in the county so far this year. In total, 82 people in the county have been treated for possible exposure to rabies in 2026. According to the Illinois Department of Public Health (IDPH), there are 13 different species of bats in Illinois. Most often, residents are likely to encounter little and big brown bats, silver-haired bats and eastern red bats. The Illinois State Veterinary Medical Association reports that between 93 and 97 percent of bats will test negative for the rabies virus. Nonetheless, they advise that a bat should be considered rabid until a test has proven otherwise. Find out what's happening in Wheaton for free with the latest updates from Patch. DCHD reports that bats are the primary source of rabies in Illinois. Bats who have been infected with rabies may: Find out what's happening in Wheaton for free with the latest updates from Patch. Be seen during the day (bats are active at twilight or after dark) Be found in unlikely places, including on lawns or inside homes Be unable to fly DCHD recommends the following to prevent contracting rabies from bats. Secure your home to prevent bats from getting in Avoid handling any bats in your home Contact animal control for help containing or removing bats Do not remove a bat until you have spoken with animal control Vaccinate pets for rabies Residents are advised to seek medical attention immediately if they have been bitten by a bat. Pets should be taken to a veterinarian immediately if they may have been exposed to rabies. Get more local news delivered straight to your inbox. Sign up for free Patch newsletters and alerts

Response, status and uncertainty

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Rabid bat found near North Main Street in Greenville; 1 exposed - greenvillejournal.com

News Brief
On July 31, 2026, a rabid bat was discovered near North Main Street in Greenville, resulting in one human exposure. This marks the fourth animal to test positive for rabies in Greenville County this year, as confirmed by the Department of Public Health (DPH). While no cases or deaths have been reported, the incident highlights ongoing rabies activity in the area. Public health officials have not provided additional details regarding the investigation or the status of the exposed individual.
